# Changelog

All notable changes to floDl will be documented in this file.

The format is based on [Keep a Changelog](https://keepachangelog.com/),
and this project adheres to [Semantic Versioning](https://semver.org/).

## [Unreleased]

### Changed

- **`fdl probe` reports the tools a walk-in reaches for, and every "not installed" line speaks the box's own package manager.** A box whose `join:` block names `data_source: sshfs://` or a `source.from:` transport learns about a missing sshfs, rsync or git from `fdl probe` rather than from `fdl join` refusing to dial; a door tool the block does not name is reported softly, so a box that never mounts or pulls anything is not told it is broken. The install command those messages carry, and the ones `fdl join` and `fdl publish` print, now route through one platform decider (apt, dnf with the RHEL package names, brew with its caveat, a WSL2 pointer on Windows) instead of each message spelling `sudo apt install` on its own.
- **The CPU libtorch directory now names its platform, and a download knows what platform an installed build is for.** Bare `precompiled/cpu` stays the Linux x86_64 build — every existing checkout and the compose default keep working — while Linux aarch64 installs to `cpu-aarch64` and the macOS build to `cpu-macos`, so builds for two architectures coexist in one checkout (a shared-storage cluster mixing x86 and ARM CPU hosts, or a Mac beside its Linux container) and are selected per host through the existing `.active.<case>` / cluster `arch:` machinery. Every download records `platform=<os>-<arch>` in the variant's `.arch`, and the already-installed check compares it, so fetching over another platform's build of the same version replaces it loudly instead of silently keeping the wrong binaries. On macOS with a Docker-mounted project, `fdl setup` now fetches the CONTAINER's Linux build at the host's own architecture — Apple Silicon gets Linux arm64, where it used to fetch the host's Mach-O build and point at a manual recipe (and, a generation earlier, force x86_64) — and `fdl` exports the per-arch `LIBTORCH_CPU_PATH` so the dev container mounts the right directory with no `.env` override.
- **The async overshoot budget is derived per rank instead of a flat ceiling.** The flat default (15 batches) was wrong in both directions: on a fast rank it covered a fraction of the measured reduce window — the rank idled at the barrier for the rest — while on a slow one it let work bleed past the window. Each rank's budget now covers the measured reduce duration at that rank's own step rate, recomputed as reduces are measured, bounded by one window's allocation and by the operator's ceiling (`--max-overshoot N` still pins a flat one); the convergence guard caps the budget instead of growing it, and the config default ceiling is non-binding so the derivation governs. Validated by a five-seed A/B: quality within noise, the fast rank's in-sync utilisation roughly doubled.
- **`Address already in use` on a cluster port now names the holder, and the way out.** A `/proc` walk (Linux, strictly advisory — any probe failure leaves the plain error) appends who holds the port; when the holder is PID 1 inside a container's PID namespace — where `kill -9` fails in a way that reads as a permissions problem — the hint is the `docker rm -f` that actually clears it.
- **The record stream's meta only declares the engine scalars when a record can actually carry them.** The engine observations (`anchor`, `sync_ms`, `cpu_avg_ms`, `d_raw`, `lambda_ema`) ride sub-epoch window records exclusively, but their roll-up declarations were published unconditionally — so a run without `--reports-per-epoch` streamed (and its saved dashboard archive baked) a meta promising series no record ever carried. The declaration is now gated on the tick cadence being armed; user-declared scalar reductions ride the epoch cadence and are unaffected.
- **A relative `ssh.identity_file` now resolves against the project root, in every frame that opens an ssh connection.** The value is consumed from up to three filesystem namespaces — `fdl probe` on the host, containerized dispatch inside the compose service, and the training launcher's own ssh sessions — so an absolute path could only ever be right for one of them: a container-absolute key path made `fdl @cluster probe` fail on that host with `Identity file ... not accessible` + `Permission denied (publickey)` while runs worked, and the config example used to tell you to accept that. Now fdl's own ssh resolves a relative path against its side's project root, and the cluster envelope ships the value absolutized in the frame the training command executes in (the compose mount for a `docker:` command, the checkout otherwise), so one relative spelling — e.g. the wizard's `.fdl/<farm>/` credentials or a rank container's minted key — names the same file everywhere. Absolute and `~/` paths pass through untouched.
- **`eval_fn` always scores the consensus.** The eval sibling of the checkpoint-consensus change below: under cpu-async EASGD the elected rank's own model is a blend, so every eval — periodic, requested, and the final canonical one — scored a state that exists on no canonical artifact, and the CPU boundary dispatch could additionally run steps past the reduce before the frame arrived. The CPU path now serves eval AT the reduce: the coordinator arms the elected rank before the round, and the rank scores that round's consensus — without EASGD by evaluating the post-writeback model in place, under EASGD by swapping the consensus in and restoring its blend verbatim afterwards, so a round with an eval leaves training bit-identical to a round without one (zero VRAM overhead; one transient host stash on the one elected rank). The final canonical eval adopts the rank's retained last realized consensus before scoring, making the final metric provably the same model the checkpoint bundle persists; the elected rank deliberately ends holding it. `request_eval` is served at the next reduce (sooner than the old epoch-boundary fold), eval cadence epochs are `epoch_splits` slices so single-pass runs get interior evals, and NCCL is untouched (its boundary dispatch already scored the post-collective consensus).
- **The checkpoint bundle is the run's canonical persist form, and `checkpoint_fn` always receives the consensus.** Three holes closed together on the CPU averaging path: a natural clean end persisted nothing (a single-epoch run with `save_path` set trained to completion and left no model on disk at all), the periodic checkpoint callback ran on a rank whose own model is an EASGD blend under cpu-async — never the consensus — and the cooperative `request_checkpoint` could only be served at an epoch boundary. Now, with `save_path` set, a natural clean end always writes the FINAL consensus bundle (the run tail's last realized reduce is captured, on every pacing mode), `checkpoint_every` layers periodic consensus bundles on top (each write atomically supersedes the last, so the bundle is always the latest resume point), and the user's `checkpoint_fn` fires controller-side against a CPU copy loaded from the averaged frame — giving both backends one mental model: `checkpoint_fn` receives the consensus (NCCL already did, by post-collective timing; it keeps its elected-rank fire). `request_checkpoint` is served at the next reduce. The crash-save bundle is now documented for what it is: rank-0's own model, best-effort. And managed-tier rank children no longer materialize a per-rank final model snapshot at teardown — nothing consumed it, and the run's final model is on disk in better form; the cooperative tier's `finish()` keeps returning the rank's own state, and solo runs are unchanged.
- **Checkpoint writers create the stem's parent directories, and the run prints where the stem resolves.** A `save_path` whose directory did not exist yet failed every write with ENOENT — loud, but a stem is once-per-run config and a fresh run layout is normal, so the writers now carry `mkdir -p` semantics. And because a relative stem resolves against each process's working directory (under a launcher-managed run, the command's configured cwd — not where the operator typed the command), the controller now prints the resolved absolute stem in the run's first lines, turning a misplaced-bundle surprise into a first-line read.
- **A non-f32 model buffer no longer silently disables consensus checkpoints.** The reduce ships only f32 buffers (integer counters are deterministic per-rank passthroughs), but the checkpoint writer counted ALL buffers toward its completion target, so one non-f32 buffer meant an armed checkpoint accumulated forever and never wrote — with no error. The schema now records which buffers ride the frame: bundles carry the f32 subset keyed by full-list index, and a positional load reports the non-f32 keys as missing while the model keeps its constructed values, matching the reduce's own passthrough semantics. (`ModelSchema` gained a public field; struct-literal construction sites need the new `f32_buffer_idx`.)
- **The CPU-averaging divergence signal no longer holds a full model copy per rank.** Each rank kept a steady f32 CPU copy of every parameter (~762 MB at 190M, ~4 GB at 1B — times ranks-per-host, all shrinking the one MemAvailable every data-plane budget anchors on) whose only output was the three-scalar weight-space divergence in each sync's ack. The triple now streams through the reduce itself: each tensor's pre-image is captured into a single transient just before the consensus decode overwrites it in place, and the same shared math folds the pair and drops it — identical numbers (the one-definition-across-backends guarantee is unchanged), peak marginal RAM one tensor instead of one model. On RAM-tight hosts this also closes a swap-thrash class: the steady copy was re-written every reduce window, so its pages could never stay swapped out and the whole cohort paid the stretch at the barrier.
- **The convergence guards are named after what they measure**: `TrendGuard` → `LevelGuard` (watches the divergence **level**, via the three-rises-above-threshold rule) and `MsfGuard` → `GrowthGuard` (watches the **rate** at which divergence compounds, `λ_ema = EMA((1/k_max)·log(D_t/D_{t-1}))`). Level versus rate is the whole distinction between them, and it is why only the second may cut the anchor — an axis neither old name stated. "MSF" stood for Master Stability Function, a synchronization-theory framing that the research program tested and did not confirm (flodl's ranks are non-identical, stochastic and independently perturbed, where the theory assumes identical deterministic oscillators), and it was expanded nowhere in the codebase, so `--guard msf` was undecodable from the inside. Deprecated aliases keep the old Rust names compiling; `ddp-bench`'s selector renames hard to `--guard none|level|growth`, where a stale spelling now fails in milliseconds against the declared choice list instead of after a container start. The `research/elche-msf/` record and the `msf-cadence-control*.md` design docs keep the original vocabulary on purpose, each carrying an old→new mapping note: they are dated records of the investigation, not descriptions of the current API.
- **`-q` / `--quiet` silences `docker compose`'s container-lifecycle narration**, the `Container … Creating` / `Created` pair that sits in front of every containerized command — which is nearly all of them. Opt-in rather than always-on, because seeing those lines is the confirmation that a container really was spun up (that the command did *not* run natively); `-q` is the user saying they already know. Scoped to the narration only: the container's own output (cargo's, the training binary's) is untouched, since that is the work rather than chatter about the work. The flag also survives the process boundary, falling back to the inherited `FLODL_VERBOSITY` so the `fdl` a run line invokes *inside* the container does not start narrating again. Its help line said "Suppress all non-error output", which it never did and — a wizard's prose being its prompt's context — should not; it now describes what it does.
- **`fdl <cmd> -h` now documents the flags fdl answers itself**, in an `fdl flags` section under `Entry` — where that section's "anything else is forwarded as-is" line needs the exceptions named. `--refresh-schema` existed, worked, and appeared in no `--help` at all: discoverable only by reading `dispatch.rs` or the strict-mode universals table. Its note says whether the cache maintains itself for this command or the flag is needed by hand after a rebuild (the latter only for a cargo entry without `compile: true`). An audit of every flag fdl intercepts found this was the only one missing.
- **Inline literals in help text are the shared palette's amber** rather than bold. Bold is a weight, so it competes with surrounding prose at the same colour and read as grey emphasis; a hue separates at a glance. xterm-256 index 179 is where `--cost` from `site/assets/css/flodl-tokens.css` lands, so the terminal carries the same amber as the site, the live dashboard and `fdl ui` instead of a fourth approximation — pinned by a test, since the CSS drift gate compares stylesheets and cannot see an ANSI index.
- **Help text renders the structure its doc comments were written with.** A list in a `#[derive(FdlArgs)]` doc comment reached `--help` as a run-on sentence with inline dashes ("Accepts: - `rank-N`: … - `fastest`: …") because the derive flattened every doc comment to a single paragraph before any renderer saw it; paragraph breaks and list items now survive into the schema, and inline `` `code` `` / `*emphasis*` spans are consumed into styling instead of printed as punctuation. `fdl <cmd> --help` and a binary's own `--help` now share one layout engine (`flodl_cli::help`) rather than each having its own — the derive's copy never wrapped at all, so the same option read differently depending on which entry point printed it. Options whose description wraps are separated by a blank line, because without it the next flag's first line reads as part of the previous description. A cache written by a different `fdl` is now treated as stale: the mtime references answer "did the command change" and could not answer "did the schema format change", which is exactly what this release did.
- **`ddp-bench` declares the values its enumerated flags accept.** `--guard`, `--outer-optimizer`, `--data-source`, `--tier` and `--dashboard-theme` carry `choices` (rendered as `[possible: …]`, and validated before a container starts), and `--guard` / `--outer-optimizer` now print the default their code has always applied. `--model` / `--mode` deliberately stay open — they name registries, so `--list` enumerates them rather than a literal list that would rot — and both now point at it. `--list` itself is styled like the rest of the CLI.

### Added

- **Linux ARM (aarch64) CPU support: `fdl libtorch download` repackages the PyTorch wheel.** PyTorch publishes no linux-aarch64 libtorch zip in any bucket, but the compiled CPU bits ship inside the torch wheel, self-contained (arm_compute + NVPL BLAS/LAPACK) — so the downloader fetches the wheel and flattens its `torch/{lib,include,share}` into a normal variant, where it used to refuse the platform outright. `fdl setup` carries the whole recipe on an ARM box (Graviton/Ampere servers, Apple Silicon's Linux container): detect, fetch, activate, dev image, no manual step left — the wheel-extraction snippet leaves the Apple Silicon guide. The wheel's manylinux_2_28 floor is glibc 2.28, LOWER than the x86 archives need, so the ARM path is more portable than what ships today. GPU variants keep refusing there by name: the CUDA aarch64 wheel is not self-contained (its runtime layer is 15 separate nvidia-* wheels) and no ROCm aarch64 build exists, so `fdl libtorch build` from source remains the GPU path. The OS-matrix arm leg flips from asserting the refusal to installing, compiling, running the CPU test suite and training through it — driven by `fdl setup` itself, the first leg to exercise the wizard in CI, and the only place flodl's tests meet this architecture.
- **Live GPU metrics on AMD, read from the kernel rather than a library.** flodl's per-device utilisation came from NVML alone, so on a ROCm build every device reported none: a run's own artifacts could not say what the hardware had been doing, which is exactly the question a heterogeneous cadence run exists to answer. AMD devices are now read from amdgpu's sysfs, which needs no ROCm SMI, no library and no extra install, and each device's paths are resolved once at sampler construction so a poll tick stays a plain file read. Utilisation joins the existing per-device sample immediately; temperature and power are read and exposed too, ahead of the sample fields that will carry them. Two things worth knowing: the published attributes vary by part, so the probe records what the driver actually offered and reports a missing sensor as absent rather than zero, and inside a container the kernel's GPU topology needs `--device=/dev/kfd` (the same device node ROCm itself requires, so a container that cannot read it could not have trained either). Both vendors also gained an on-demand hardware probe that prints which backend a box resolved, since a suite that must pass on a GPU-less machine can never assert that metrics are live.
- **The dashboard grew tabs, and the timeline is one of them.** A tab strip on the navigation row — Overview, Architecture, Timeline — declutters the main page: the graph SVG and its heat-map views move into Architecture, and the new Timeline tab embeds the forensic timeline, crumb-aware (root shows the controller's timeline, `root/<host>` that host's). Live, it reads each host's shipped telemetry stream uniformly — the controller's included — auto-refreshing on the shipper's clock (~10s, zoom and toggle choices survive a refresh); in a saved archive the tab carries the per-host page links. Tab state rides the URL hash beside the path, so deep links keep both. Served through `fdl ui`, the `/timeline*` routes proxy like the rest of the dashboard. Saved archives navigate the same way — the tab embeds each host's shipped page as you move through the crumbs — and a host that could not publish (an unwritable shared run dir) says so and points at the controller timeline instead of implying data is on the way. The Architecture tab retires the old collapse arrow: the graph opens expanded, viewport-capped, scrolling internally.
- **The live dashboard now plots the engine.** The root window record carries curated point observations of the training engine — the ElChe `anchor`, `sync_ms` / `cpu_avg_ms` round-trips, the divergence `d_raw` and its `lambda_ema` growth proxy — at the existing `--reports-per-epoch` cadence, so the metrics chart answers "what was the engine doing" live, not just post-hoc in the timeline. They are declared `last` in the stream's `meta` (they are snapshots, not rollups; a mode that lacks one contributes no key), and the record schema note lives in the monitoring design doc.
- **The dashboard archive indexes the run's forensic timelines.** Every rank process bakes its own self-contained `timeline.html` into its telemetry dir at teardown, the shipper publishes it beside the JSONL mirror, and the archive bake discovers whatever landed (`telemetry/<host>/<run>/timeline.html` beside the archive) and renders a per-host card of relative links. No control-plane plumbing: the storage fabric is the index, and a host still shipping when the archive baked is simply absent rather than a broken link. Under `fdl ui`, archives serve through a path-preserving `/archive/<path>` route so those relative links resolve to their sibling files (a query-shaped URL gave the browser no base).
- **`timeline.html` rebuilt around what the timeline actually records.** The old page drew a GPU heat strip and two resource lanes and left the rest in the JSON. The new page shows every series: per-GPU utilization and VRAM curves with idle gaps and sync windows shaded, the ElChe anchor trajectory, sync durations, divergence and its growth rate, loss and learning rate, host CPU/RAM, and the rank-reported samples of remote hosts (previously never injected, so cluster runs lost every remote GPU in the visual). Per-pixel min/max envelope decimation keeps spikes visible at any zoom, drag/wheel zoom with a linked cursor replaces the slider pair, and the shared design tokens + theme toggle make it the dashboard's sibling. Each event-kind toggle governs everything derived from that kind — its strips, its span shading, and its event ticks — so every click has a visible effect. Series colors are CVD-validated per co-drawn group.
- **Telemetry trickles to shared storage instead of bursting at teardown.** `TelemetryShipper` mirrors a node-local telemetry dir to a destination (the shared run dir in cluster use) on its own clock: `*.log` files are followed as streams, so the local ring's rotation stays invisible and the shared copy keeps the run's full history; appends land in `.partial` files that publish by rename at teardown, and a `.partial` left behind honestly marks an unpublished mirror. Contained by construction — destination errors warn and retry, and a stalled shared mount strands the shipper thread alone: `finish` is time-bounded and never hangs teardown. `ddp-bench` ships each process's spill to `<run dir>/telemetry/<host>/<run>/`, and the record log (`record_log_dir`) rides the same plane: the writer stages on node-local disk and the shipper mirrors it to the configured directory, so a shared destination can never stall a record append.
- **Node-local timeline spill: the forensic timeline now survives the crash it exists to explain.** `Timeline`'s full-resolution archive was RAM-only, so a dying run took its own diagnostics with it. `TimelineSpill` consumes the timeline's broadcast on its own thread and appends every sample and event to a bounded JSONL ring under `~/.flodl/telemetry/<run>/`, with the record log's discipline: drop-oldest segments, I/O errors swallowed, disk never on the training thread. Spill lines share `timeline.json`'s object shapes, so one parser serves both, and rank-reported samples now ride the broadcast so a controller-side sink also sees remote hosts. `ddp-bench` spills per process and prints the directory in its end-of-run summary.
- **`fdl ui` — the local operations page.** One loopback web page for the project's farms, hardware probe, cluster run status and resolved config — the browser counterpart of the walk-in CLI surface — plus the first actions: the join-config wizard as a preview-then-apply form (Preview runs `--dry-run` and Apply unlocks only after a preview of exactly that form state, so the dry run IS the confirmation step; the applied report carries the authorized_keys line and worker yml with copy buttons, while cloud-init stays a file path because a secret artifact is never served over HTTP), and publish with its gate build streamed live line by line (one job at a time, since concurrent publishes would race the manifest commit point; a closed tab never kills the build, and "Follow last job" replays the stream and follows). The page drives the CLI, never reimplements it: every panel spawns `fdl` itself with `--json` and renders argv + exit + output verbatim, with the exact command line (copyable) above each result, so anything done in the browser is reproducible in a terminal and the page structurally cannot drift from the CLI. Zero dependencies (hand-rolled HTTP over `std::net`, the embedded page self-contained). Binds 127.0.0.1 only; on top of the bind, a Host-header check refuses DNS-rebinding pages, a per-session token (minted from OS entropy, injected into the served page, required on every API route) refuses blind cross-site requests, mutations are POST-only, and every form value is charset-gated before it becomes argv (a flag-shaped value is refused, so a form field cannot inject options). From another box: `ssh -L 1338:127.0.0.1:1338 <controller>`, the same trust story as the cluster.
- **`fdl ui` job streams self-heal.** A heartbeat keeps an idle stream alive through whatever sits between the page and the server, the buffer is a drop-oldest ring that says how many lines fell out instead of silently starting late, and every buffered line carries its absolute stream index, so a dropped tab or proxy hop resumes exactly where it died and the page auto-reconnects. The stream opens by naming its job, and a resume cursor is honored only against the job that minted it — a reconnect that raced a NEW job into the slot replays that job from its start, with a note, rather than skipping its head.
- **`fdl ui` consoles honour the command's own colours.** Output was coloured by STREAM, which was actively misleading: cargo writes its whole progress narration to stderr, so every ordinary `Compiling …` line rendered amber — the palette's hazard colour — while real errors looked identical. Streamed children now run with the conventional colour-forcing env (`FORCE_COLOR`, `CLICOLOR_FORCE`, `CARGO_TERM_COLOR`) and the page renders the ANSI they emit: the 8 colours plus bold/dim/italic/underline, 256-colour and truecolour, with every non-SGR escape (cursor moves, erase-line, OSC) dropped rather than printed as mojibake and `\r` resolved to the line's final revision. fdl ui's own voice (a lost stream, a buffer gap, a failed spawn) keeps the amber, now marked as its own stream rather than inferred from a file descriptor. Every driven command asks for colour, not only the streamed ones: a result pane is a human reading output too, and `fdl config show` renders its keys and per-key provenance in colour a pipe would otherwise discard. Safe unconditionally because fdl never styles a JSON body — the styling lives on the human paths and on stderr, both of which the page reads through the same ANSI renderer — and in those one-answer panes stderr keeps its amber, since there it really is the diagnostic channel.
- **Colour-forcing env vars now cross the docker boundary** (`FORCE_COLOR`, `CLICOLOR_FORCE`, `CARGO_TERM_COLOR`, forwarded by name only when set, so ordinary use is untouched and no value lands on a command line). A tool inside the container decides on colour from its own stdout, so anything capturing fdl's output — CI logs as much as `fdl ui` — previously lost every colour at that boundary: `FORCE_COLOR=1 fdl test` did what its author obviously meant on the host and nothing at all inside the image.
- **`fdl ui` carries fdl's global options and a help button.** Verbosity (`-q` / `-v` / `-vv` / `-vvv`), `--gpus`, `--no-append` and `--no-prebuild` are grouped with the env select in the launch tab — the same fdl-level scope, and like `--env` they precede the command; being nobody's schema, they were previously unreachable from the page. They travel as structured fields, so a body cannot smuggle an arbitrary pre-command argument, and a re-launch recovers the globals the record carries rather than applying today's form state. The new help button runs the command with `--help` (the only way to see a schema-less command's surface from the browser); it streams like a launch but is kept out of the run ledger, decided from the argv rather than trusted from the body, so a real launch cannot opt itself out of history.
- **`fdl ui` layout and schema-resolution fixes.** A command's schema is resolved the way the CLI resolves it — through the command's own directory (cached `--fdl-schema` output under `<cmd>/.fdl/schema-cache/`, or an inline `schema:` block), not a nonexistent root-level cache, so launch forms now appear for the commands that have a surface (`ddp-bench`'s 59 options among them) instead of every command claiming none; the load stays the cheap one, so a page view never triggers a cargo compile, and the "no form" hint distinguishes a shell `run:` command (never grows one) from a stale cache (`--refresh-schema`). The run ledger moved from the history tab to a scrolling column beside the launch form, where clicking an entry proposes re-launching that exact recorded argv behind a confirm; the history tab is now training history alone. And a tab's data loads whenever the tab is entered, so a reload or a shared `#tab` link no longer paints an empty panel that only fills after clicking away and back.
- **`fdl ui` launch tab: run the project's configured commands from the browser.** The menu is the `fdl.yml` `commands:` tree under the selected farm overlay (where a farm's `cluster: true` run command lives); a command implementing the `--fdl-schema` contract gets a form generated from its cached schema (bools as checkboxes, choices as selects, defaults as hints — only fields the operator sets become argv, defaults stay the binary's own), and no cached schema degrades to a freeform args field rather than a blocker. Launches stream live through the same one-slot job machinery as publish, a `--monitor <port>` in the args points the run tab's dashboard slot at the run automatically, and each completed launch appends one line to the run ledger (`.fdl/ui/runs.jsonl`: ts, duration, farm, exact argv, exit) that the history tab lists. The launch surface drives only commands declared in the project's config, never arbitrary fdl subcommands; a failed spawn is streamed but never enters the ledger (nothing ran). Deliberately no stop button: a killed cluster run leaves ports held and ranks spinning, and a button pretending otherwise would lie.
- **`fdl ui` run and history tabs.** The run tab is one slot whose backing follows the run's lifecycle: the admission view (`fdl status`, quietly re-probing) until the run's dashboard answers, then the live dashboard itself, reverse-proxied through the ui's own port — one `ssh -L` forward carries the whole experience on a headless controller. The proxy forwards the dashboard's root-relative routes verbatim and only ever to a loopback port (the host is not configurable, so it cannot be aimed off-box); its SSE legs get no upstream read timeout on purpose. The history tab lists persisted dashboards discovered on disk (newest first, rustdoc's `timeline.rs.html` lookalikes and hidden/build trees excluded — both false-positive classes were found live before the predicate learned them) and serves them into the same slot; archive serving is double-bounded (inside the project root AND named like a run artifact). Also: one design-token palette now has a canonical home (`site/assets/css/flodl-tokens.css`), vendored verbatim into the ui page between marker comments with `ci/release/13-design-tokens.sh` failing on drift; the dashboard and site adopt it in their own passes.
- **A run publishes where its dashboard is, so `fdl ui` can embed it without being told.** The launcher's dashboard sink binds on the first rank's register frame, on a port only rank user-code knew; it now reports that port to the run's status board, which re-renders the current `state.json` immediately rather than waiting for a membership transition that may never come (the bind happens after the last one). `fdl status` prints the resulting `dashboard: host:port` line, and `fdl ui`'s run tab reads the same document it already fetches for the admission view, so connecting to a cluster that was **already running** now embeds its live dashboard instead of asking the operator to know the port. Discovery only proposes: the loopback reachability probe still decides, because the ui proxies 127.0.0.1 only and the run may be on another box — and a reported-but-unreachable port renders the exact `ssh -L` forward rather than silently proxying whatever local service happens to sit on that number. `dashboard_port` is always present in the document (null when the run serves none) so a consumer can tell "no dashboard" from "older flodl". No wire change: `state.json` is HTTP, and the field is the status document's, not `MembershipSnapshot`'s, since the membership ledger has no business knowing about dashboards.
- **`fdl cargo` — report and reclaim cargo's on-disk footprint.** Bare `fdl cargo` sizes both tiers per path (a long-lived checkout quietly accumulates tens of GB); `fdl cargo target --clear` reclaims compiled artifacts (`target/`, `.target*`, workspace-excluded crates' `target/` — cost: recompute, no network) and `fdl cargo cache --clear` reclaims the registry caches (`.cargo-cache*`, `.cargo-git*` — cost: re-download). The split is the reclaim-cost axis, which is why the bare command refuses `--clear`: clearing names the cost you accept. Clearing empties contents and always keeps the directories — several are docker bind-mount sources, and a removed source comes back root-owned on the next compose run, breaking container builds. Undeletable entries are skipped and reported with the bytes they still hold plus a working `sudo find … -mindepth 1 -delete` hint (absolute, and a form that keeps the mount source), never fatal. The one service that produced such entries is fixed at the source in the same pass: `docs-rs` now carries the same `user:` host-identity mapping as every other build service, so it stops writing root-owned files into the three directories it mounts. `--json` on all three surfaces.
- **`fdl join-config --list` and `--dry-run`**, the wizard's read-only companions. `--list` enumerates the project's farms — the union of `fdl.<label>.*` overlays and `.fdl/<label>/` farm dirs — with door, controller and credential state; env overlays that are not farms are reported apart rather than dressed as broken ones, and non-farm `.fdl/` state (schema caches) never lists. `--dry-run` runs the full pass with every write withheld and reports what an apply would `create`, `update` or leave alone, per file; credentials an apply would mint appear as placeholders rather than values the apply would not reproduce. Neither ever prompts (a dry run reads the consent flags exactly as given), both combine with `--json`, and every wizard write now goes through one recorder, so an idle re-run rewrites nothing and the applied report carries the same per-file change list a dry run promises. First consumers: scripts today, the `fdl ui` local web page next.
- **`fdl clippy` now runs `cargo fmt --all --check` first** (fdl.yml.example): formatting and lints are one style gate, so the habitual local gate can no longer pass on a tree CI will refuse; `fdl fmt` writes the fix.
- **The graph timing heat map, rendered.** The launcher's dashboard bakes the per-rank timing frames into finished SVGs — one per GPU model — by recolouring the structural graph SVG it already holds: no `dot` at render time (the reason the ranks ship data, not pictures, is that graphviz is not a runtime dependency, and the controller needs it no more than they do), each node heat-filled by its relative mean with min/mean in the native hover tooltip, and a legend band carrying GPU model, rank count, samples, clock provenance and the pass mean. The Graph Architecture card grows a per-model switcher plus an SVG download that saves **exactly the bytes on screen** — the display IS the artifact, so a publication figure cannot diverge from what was reviewed — and the saved HTML archive embeds the same finished SVGs, so the artifact survives offline. Aggregation is sample-weighted mean-of-means and min-of-mins within a GPU model, never across models — nor across clocks: a rank whose event profiling degraded to the host clock bakes its own map rather than blending launch times into siblings' execution times. Solo runs are untouched: `svg_with_profile` keeps its dot-rendered path, and both read on the same colour scale.
- **`TrainerConfig::profile_graph` — the timing heat map's data plane.** When set, every rank's factory-built training graph is profiled with device-side events and the accumulated per-node min/mean ships to the controller's dashboard sink in one frame at clean teardown (teardown rather than "last epoch", so convergence shutdowns, save-and-stop and elastic scale-down still yield the picture; error exits skip it — the device may hold an aborted collective). Symmetric across ranks by construction — the config reaches every rank — which is what keeps ElChe's delivered-cost scheduling undisturbed; overhead is one event record per node per pass. The frame carries the graph's structural hash (the sink refuses a mismatched cohort loudly instead of averaging incomparable graphs) and the rank's GPU model name, the aggregation key: averaging within a model is legitimate, a cross-model mean describes no device that exists. Eval and epoch-callback forwards are excluded from the accumulator — they fire on one elected rank and would tilt exactly that rank's means. No-op when the model is not a `Graph`; `ddp-bench` derives it from `--save-dashboard` (the heat map's only consumer is the dashboard artifact). Rendering lands separately; the wire change bumps `CONTROL_PROTOCOL_VERSION` to 5.
- **`Graph::profile_stats()` — per-node min/mean accumulated across profiled passes.** The single-pass `profile()` answers "what did the last pass cost"; the accumulator answers the two questions a timing heat map is actually built from: the minimum across passes (the standard estimator of a node's *intrinsic* cost, the sample least polluted by reduce barriers, data starve or allocator growth) and the mean (what it costs in practice — mean alone gets dragged by outliers and makes contention read as expense). The first 3 profiled passes are skipped as warmup (cudnn algorithm selection settles by then), a device move resets the accumulator (host-clock and device-event samples must not average together), and the readout carries `structural_hash` + `ProfileSource` so downstream cross-rank aggregation keyed by node index can refuse incomparable graphs by name. Feeds from both clocks; same pull semantics as `profile()`.

### Fixed

- **CPU averaging measured its sync time from the wrong signal, which stalled anchor growth.** A rank's next batch report was read as the reduce completing, but under cpu-async the ranks train THROUGH the averaging, so the clock stopped about one batch in: ElChe was fed ~200ms against a measured 13.8s round-trip, per-window overhead read as negligible, and the anchor never grew. Completion now gates on each rank's divergence evidence — the signal the finalize step already trusted — and the measured duration spans the whole rendezvous, so anchors reach their cap unaided and the overhead tuning sees the true cost. NCCL keeps its ack-based capture, which was never wrong there.
- **`fdl <cmd> -h` had silently lost every binary-backed option list.** The schema probe wrapped the raw `docker:` value while the exec path resolved it, so a logical service name made every probe die in milliseconds — and a probe error is a documented fall-through, so help just quietly rendered no options. The probe now reproduces the exec path's resolution (libtorch env included), its compile cost is charged only to the surfaces that need a schema (help render, exec validation) so a page view or tab-press never spins a container, and a stale cache is served rather than dropped.
- **The end-of-run consensus reduce can no longer be skipped by a message-drain race — and when forced, it arms the final bundle again.** Two layers on one race, both surfacing only on loaded CI boxes. First: the coordinator's shutdown decision reads a trailing-steps view fed by the timing drain at the top of the tick, while the epoch aggregate that triggers the decision drains at the bottom; a rank's last step report and its epoch end both landing inside the tick body (a descheduled coordinator) made the decision read zero trailing steps and shut down without the forced final reduce — silently: the cohort ended un-reduced, the final eval scored a one-window-stale consensus, and the natural-end bundle above was never armed. The decision now re-drains timing first, which makes it exact: the aggregate requires every live rank's epoch end, and each rank's step reports precede its epoch end on the same connection. Second, exposed by the first fix: the forced reduce runs post-aggregate, by which point the aggregated epoch's chunk pool has been removed — and the bundle-arm's tail check misread the missing pool as "final epoch not dispatched yet", so the forced reduce ran coherently but never armed the write. An aggregated epoch now counts as the run's tail unconditionally. The checkpoint forge also carries forensic counters (arms taken, writers spawned) so a missing artifact names which stage went silent, and a worker whose control channel disconnects without a `Shutdown` says so now, instead of producing the same clean exit as a natural end, minus its artifacts.
- **Graph profiling on CUDA now times execution, not the launch.** The profiler was a host clock around each node call while CUDA launches are async, so a profiled forward saw only the enqueue — on this fix's validation probe (21-conv stack) the launch was 0.29ms against a 1.67ms drained pass, with the shortfall attributed to whichever node hit launch-queue backpressure rather than the node doing the work. GPU forwards now bracket every node with pooled device-side boundary events: N+1 markers, one before the first node and one after each, so the deltas telescope and the node sum equals the pass total. Timings resolve one pass behind the forward calls and the training loop never synchronizes for them; a `profile()` read serves the freshest drained pass and waits only when there is nothing else to serve (the first read after a single profiled pass). `Profile` now says which clock produced it (`source: ProfileSource` — `GpuEvents` or `HostWallClock`), surfaced in `Display` and the SVG legend, so device and host numbers can no longer be conflated silently; the new field is the one compile-visible change for code constructing `Profile` literally. One reading changes honestly rather than regressively: `LevelTiming::parallelism()` is 1.0 by construction on GPU, because nodes execute sequentially on one stream — the metric previously measured host-loop overhead, not device concurrency.
- **`fdl join-config` no longer emits a daemon-level guardrail for a door on port 22.** The sshd drop-in scopes its restrictions with `Match LocalPort <door>`, which is what keeps ordinary logins untouched when the door has its own port; for a controller declared on port 22 the same block would have applied `PermitTTY no` and a single permitted forward to every login on the box, the operator's own included, under a header promising that 22 stayed alone. The generated file now says why nothing is installed and points at a dedicated port for the daemon half; the key line carries the whole guardrail on 22, as it always did.

## [0.8.0] - 2026-08-09

> Upgrading from 0.7.0? Most of the surface moved by rename with a
> deprecated alias left behind, so existing code keeps compiling and
> starts warning. Two changes do not warn: the Rust floor is now **1.91**,
> and `flodl-hf` needs **owner-qualified Hub repo ids** (`bert-base-uncased`
> becomes `google-bert/bert-base-uncased`) because hf-hub 1.0 no longer
> follows the Hub's redirect. See [UPGRADE.md](UPGRADE.md) for the
> step-by-step migration.

### Added

- **AMD (ROCm) GPU support.** `flodl-sys` compiles and links against a hipified libtorch. The vendor is a build-time property (libtorch ships one backend per build) and is reconciled in `flodl-sys/gpu_compat.h`, which maps the shim's CUDA spelling onto `c10/hip` + `ATen/hip` and routes streams through `HIPStreamMasqueradingAsCUDA`. Not yet validated on AMD hardware: this covers compile, link, provisioning and detection only. Provisioning is vendor-plural throughout, including the parts that only a second vendor makes visible: cluster fan-out counts a worker's GPUs for both vendors in one round trip (`nvidia-smi` for NVIDIA, the KFD topology's `vendor_id 4098` for AMD, which is mask-proof) and reduces them with that host's declared `arch:`, since one libtorch build serves one vendor and that field already names which; an undeclared host reporting both vendors is refused by name rather than guessed at, because the controller cannot know which build it will load and a guess assigns ranks to devices nobody addresses.
- **`flodl-hw`, a dependency-free hardware-detection crate** shared by `flodl` and `flodl-cli` (`flodl::sys` and `flodl_cli::util::system` are facades over it). AMD devices are read from the kernel's KFD topology, and reported as usable devices only when a ROCm userspace runtime is present; otherwise they surface as a finding naming the arch and what to install.
- **`fdl libtorch download --rocm 7.0` and `--rocm 7.1`.** `--path`, `--no-activate` and `--dry-run` are now listed in `fdl libtorch -h`. The two ROCm builds cover exactly the same gfx targets, so the choice is about the runtime, not the hardware: the HIP ordering rule puts the host's own ROCm ahead of the bundled one, and within a major version that ABI only grows, so a bundle older than the host loads while a newer one can fail on a symbol the host does not have. Auto-detection therefore routes to 7.0, which serves every 7.x host; a box that wants the exact match asks for it by version. Picking the newest bundle the detected host runtime can satisfy is the better rule and waits on ROCm version resolution. The covered gfx list is read off the published archive's own index rather than inferred: `gfx908`, `gfx90a`, `gfx942`, `gfx950`, `gfx1030`, `gfx1100`, `gfx1101`, `gfx1102`, `gfx1150`, `gfx1151`, `gfx1200` and `gfx1201` ship rocBLAS kernels, while `gfx900` and `gfx906` (Vega 10 and Vega 20, i.e. MI50 and Radeon VII) carry MIOpen tuning databases and no kernels at all, so they count as uncovered instead of being installed and left to fail at the first matmul. Both ROCm buckets carry the same set. Upstream publishes ROCm builds for Linux only, so `--rocm` is refused on Windows and macOS with that reason.
- **RHEL-family install guidance.** The missing-toolkit message from `flodl-sys`'s build script now prints a `RHEL/Fedora: sudo dnf install …` line beside the Ubuntu one, and `fdl probe`/`fdl setup` pick the dnf spelling on RHEL-family hosts (detected via `/etc/os-release`). The rpm names are a mechanical transform of the Debian ones (`-dev` → `-devel`, `g++` → `gcc-c++`), every spelling verified against the vendors' rhel9 repositories: NVIDIA's `cuda-rhel9` repo and AMD's `rocm/rhel9` repo, whose setup one-liners the CI toolkit phase now exercises.
- **`fdl probe` reports a libtorch variant this host's dynamic linker cannot load.** Upstream does not build every archive against the same C library baseline: measured on 2.10.0, the cpu and cu128 trees need `GLIBC_2.29` while the rocm7.0 tree needs `GLIBC_2.35`. RHEL 9 ships glibc 2.34 and cannot move past it (libstdc++ is upgradable through `gcc-toolset`, glibc is not, since the whole userland links against it), so that pair downloads, compiles, links, and then dies in the loader quoting symbol versions. The probe asks `ldd` and names the unmet versions with what they mean, before any of that is spent. Found by the OS matrix's rocky leg on its ROCm rotation; `Dockerfile.rhel` documents the baselines as a buildable reference.
- **`fdl probe` and `fdl setup` report missing prerequisites with the command to install them**: host tools (curl or wget, unzip, a C++ compiler) and the vendor toolkit headers for the active libtorch variant. Presence is decided by capability, not by a list of known paths: when the header is in neither the toolkit root nor the default system include directories, the check asks the compiler that will do the build, with the same `-I` it will get, so a box carrying its headers somewhere unusual (or reaching them through `CPATH` or a multiarch directory) is not told to install what it already has. `flodl-sys`'s build gate shares the behaviour, so a missing header now names the packages to install instead of failing inside the C++ compile (libtorch bundles the runtime libraries, so headers are the only gap). The command is rendered per family throughout, so a RHEL host is told `sudo dnf install hip-devel …` rather than apt with Debian package names. The probe runs only for a header already believed missing, so an ordinary build spawns nothing, and "no compiler to ask" stays a distinct answer from "missing". Setup reports the native prerequisites only when the build is native.
- **Per-OS CI matrix** (`ci/os-matrix.sh`, `.github/workflows/os.yml`) covering Linux x86_64 and aarch64, macOS arm64 and Windows x86_64. Each host installs libtorch through `fdl` and builds against it; the Linux leg alternates CUDA and ROCm.
- **A RHEL-family box you can actually open** (`Dockerfile.rhel`, the `rhel` compose service, commented `rhel-*` commands in `fdl.yml`). Now that the tool gives RHEL-specific advice, a RHEL question should be answerable on a RHEL userland rather than by reasoning about one: the dnf package names, `rrsync` shipping as non-executable documentation, `curl-minimal` conflicting with `curl`, and which libtorch baselines EL9 can load. Deliberately not what CI uses — the rocky legs run a stock image and install prerequisites the way the docs tell a user to, which is the step a pre-baked image would skip.
- **`ci/rig-ladder.sh`, a multi-GPU validation ladder that cannot report a false pass.** The NCCL suites are device-gated (`require_multi_gpu()` early-returns), so on a one-GPU box the whole suite reports `ok` in a tenth of a second having tested nothing. The ladder answers what a rig actually EXECUTED: every rung reports RAN or SKIPPED with its reason, a pass requires a zero exit AND the trainer's `done:` line AND no libtorch `[W]` warnings, and a ladder where nothing ran exits non-zero rather than looking green. Three rungs: single-host process-per-rank, heterogeneous cross-host fan-out, and a walk-in dialing a guardrailed door (controller here, worker joins, `fdl start` fires the freeze).
- **CI runs what it used to only compile.** The CUDA and ROCm jobs now RUN flodl's lib suite on their GPU-less runners (the driverless deployment a `--features cuda`/`rocm` build meets on any CPU-only box, and it immediately caught two driver-assuming tests); the CPU job asserts the model-sig probe wire contract (the `flodl-model-sig:` line, exit before training, identical hex across runs) and feeds `fdl join-config --cloud-init`'s artifact through a real YAML parser; and the OS matrix gained a scaffold smoke: `fdl init --native`, the registry-pin regression guard, then an env-scrubbed `./fdl build` and `./fdl run` against the checkout's flodl, proving the scaffold's printed next steps on every compiling host. A rockylinux:9 job (host + `docker run`, because a `container:` job cannot free host disk, and the rocm rotation needs ~30 GB) runs the same script on the OPPOSITE rotation phase from the ubuntu leg, so every push exercises both vendors, one per distro family; `FDL_CI_VARIANT` gained `cpu` and `rotate-alt` arms. The matrix covers both ends of the supported range rather than only one: Ubuntu 24.04 (the base releases are built against, so the floor users have) alongside 26.04, and two EL releases, each given only variants it can actually run — EL9's glibc 2.34 cannot load the rocm archive at all, so it takes the cpu leg while EL10 (glibc 2.39) carries the RHEL-family vendor rotation. Scheduling a combination that cannot work is not a test, it is a failure that never heals.
- **`fdl coverage` and `fdl coverage-all`, a coverage report that names what it could not run.** `coverage` is the CPU one-liner and therefore a FLOOR, scoring every GPU-reachable region as missed; `coverage-all` (`ci/coverage.sh`) drives the phases itself across the containers they each need, and prints a `RAN` / `SKIPPED` / `FAILED` roster rather than one number. The roster is the point, and it is the same hazard the rig ladder answers: a hardware- or tool-gated suite returns early and reports `ok`, so a single percentage can be built entirely out of suites that executed nothing. Coverage here is a number to look at, deliberately never a threshold to clear: the I/O glue in `flodl-cli` is uncovered on purpose (mocking `Command` / fs / tty is testing the mock), so a floor would push toward exactly the tests this project decided not to write. `--skip leakcheck` is required rather than tidy: those tests assert RSS growth stays under a bound, and the instrumentation's own counters allocate.
- **`fdl fmt` and `fdl fmt-check`.** Formatting became a CI gate with the rustfmt adoption below, and a gate with no local command means contributors meet it for the first time as a red build. `fmt` writes, `fmt-check` is byte-for-byte the check CI runs.
- **`ci/release/11-semver-checks.sh`**: public-API breakage checked against what is actually published, per crate, automating a step that was being done by eye. It catches a class the test suite structurally cannot, since all 3000 tests compile against the current source and none of them can notice that a name a downstream crate imports has gone. It runs after `02-version-sync.sh` deliberately: the question is whether the DECLARED version covers the changes made, so running it before the bump correctly fails. Needs no libtorch (`DOCS_RS=1`, and rustdoc never links); absent tooling prints `UNVERIFIED` rather than passing quietly.
- **Discovery windows: a cluster can now form with no roster at all, because `controller.join.discovery: true` makes the join window itself define the world.** `workers:` may be empty; whoever walks in during the window (see `fdl join` below) IS the topology, ranks assigned in admission order exactly as dial-in membership always did (the cloud shape, where worker addresses do not exist before the VMs boot). Discovery requires an explicit `min_rank_start` (there is no configured capacity to derive a quorum from) and leaves the early-close target unset unless asked, so the window stays open its full length for stragglers. Fan-out and discovery compose: an enumerated rig fans out as usual while walk-ins self-register into the same window. Two trust knobs round out the admission story: `join.token` is a pre-shared session salt (hex) replacing the per-run generated one, so a credential injected at fleet-create time can be presented by walk-ins; it forces credential-checked admission even behind sshd (the guardrails and the token are layers, not alternatives) and contradicts `open_admission: true` loudly; `join.tunnel_only` binds the controller loopback-only with no roster to infer that from, making an sshd-carried forward the ONLY road in: reachability itself is the authentication (CPU averaging modes only, like any tunneled worker). Walk-ins admitted through a loopback bind synthesize `tunnel: true` so their rank children dial the loopback end of the forward rather than an unreachable config address.
- **The staging hold: `controller.join.start: manual` (or `hybrid`) turns the join window into an operator surface, and `fdl start` fires it.** Auto stays the default: the clock closes the window (target / expiry) as always. Under manual, quorum met puts the run in a new `staging` phase (visible in `fdl status`, which renders "roster startable, fire with `fdl start`"): the roster is held, window expiry keeps holding, and only the operator's start (or the hard cap, loudly, with a raise-the-cap hint) ends the hold; `target_ranks` is refused up front since a closing target contradicts an operator-owned close. Hybrid keeps both: the clock still closes, and the operator may fire earlier. This is the scavenged-credit shape: launch instances until the money runs out, watch `fdl status`, start when the roster looks full enough. The switch itself is an authenticated `POST /start` on the same single training port (the mux routes the request line's `POST` like a channel magic): from the controller box or through the sshd tunnel the loopback peer address is the credential, from anywhere else `?token=` must match the run's session salt. Refusals name their reason (auto mode, quorum not met with counts, window already closed), and arming below quorum is refused rather than queued; the verdict loop stays the authority (quorum is re-checked at the fire poll).
- **`fdl join`: the worker-side walk-in.** `fdl join [<controller>] --token <hex> --bin <training-binary> -- <its args>` dials a window, offers the box's GPUs (`--devices` to scope), and runs the binary in agent role: it joins, then spawns and supervises the host's relay and rank children itself, byte-identical to the fan-out path from there. `--ssh [user@]host[:port]` brings up a local `ssh -L` forward of the controller port (fresh per attempt, `ExitOnForwardFailure`, never a password prompt) and dials through it, the guardrailed-sshd trust path; the agent bootstrap spec rides the child's environment, never argv, the same salt hygiene as fan-out. Every flag defaults from a top-level `join:` block in fdl.yml so a golden image boots into bare `fdl join`, and `--persist` re-dials with backoff (5s doubling to 60s) whenever the agent exits (no window yet, run finished, controller rebooted), so a fleet can sit ready before the operator ever launches. When fdl runs inside a project, the active libtorch's `lib/` rides `LD_LIBRARY_PATH` onto the binary automatically (`FDL_LIBTORCH_CASE` honored) and its variant label rides the join hello. Two structural pieces landed with it: worker agents now rewrite the controller address in their formed-world artifacts (envelope + relay spec) with the address their own join provably reached, because a walk-in's ephemeral-port tunnel or a NAT'd controller makes the controller-authored address plain wrong on that host, and the relay dying on it used to take the whole host down; and the launcher learned that walk-in workers are nobody's process children, so run completion now waits on every host control link's EOF (a discovery cohort can have ZERO process children, and supervision returning instantly used to tear the world down moments after forming it), while a failure verdict actively sends each connected host an Abort down that link instead of leaving agents nobody told to die.
- **The join sshd guardrail recipe** (docs/ddp/02-cluster-guide.md): what to put in front of a controller that accepts walk-ins from outside the private network. One authorized_keys line, `restrict,port-forwarding,permitopen="127.0.0.1:1337",command="/usr/sbin/nologin"`, reduces a compromised join key to exactly one capability: a port forward to the controller mux and nothing else (no shell, no pty, no agent forwarding, no other destination; verified live: the same reachable port answers directly and returns nothing through the restricted forward). The forced command covers *subsystem* requests too, so that line also refuses sftp and rsync while leaving `ssh -N` untouched, so the recipe now spells out what a key must carry when the same box also serves the walk-in's data mount (`internal-sftp -R`, read-only but scoped only by the user unless chrooted) or a source pull (`rrsync -ro`, directory-scoped), and that one key cannot do both. For permanent setups the same restrictions repeat at the daemon level under a dedicated no-shell user (`Match User` block) so a mistake in either layer is caught by the other; the ephemeral variant is a dedicated sshd instance whose lifetime brackets the run: own config, own port, an authorized_keys carrying only join keys, no root needed above 1024. One asymmetry the recipe spells out: established forwards ARE the tunneled workers' transport, so bounding the window means disabling new admissions after formation, and tearing the sshd down only once the run is over.

- **`fdl join` prepares the box before it dials, and classes failures by what retrying can fix.** Preparation runs per attempt, strictly before the tunnel, because admission starts a window deadline: a GPU gate (no usable device means no dial: the agent's own check comes only after admission, when the host is already counted into a quorum and its failure takes the cohort's formation with it), the dataset source root (`join.data_path`, and `join.data_source: sshfs://[user@]host[:port]/abs/path` to mount it there, READ-ONLY, so the kernel enforces the ranks-never-write-the-source-root invariant instead of flodl merely obeying it; an already-mounted root is reused), and write-proofs of the node-local directories the data plane uses (the `~/.flodl/data` cache and the temp stage, proven by writing: permissions, ACLs, a full disk and a read-only mount all present identically to a write). Transient failures (controller not up, mount attempt, tunnel) exit **1** and re-dial under `--persist`; permanent ones (no GPU, an unparseable spec, a missing tool, an unwritable directory) exit **2** and do not, so a misprovisioned instance stops instead of hot-looping. fdl never powers a box off itself: the shipped systemd recipe pairs 2 with `RestartPreventExitStatus=2` + `FailureAction=poweroff`, and the recipe is written for `--persist` (one-shot mode passes the agent's own exit code through, so 2 is reserved there).
- **A walk-in can build its training binary instead of being handed one** (`join.source:` + `join.libtorch:`). The tree always lands on LOCAL disk first (cargo stats every source file on every invocation, so a mount build pays that latency thousands of times, and the attribute caching that would hide it makes cargo serve a stale binary), with mtimes preserved so a re-dial costs the changed files and nothing else; the build links against the exact libtorch the box holds, which makes the ABI match by construction rather than by manifest discipline. Three transports, and the scheme names the tool: `file:///abs/path`, `rsync://[user@]host[:port]:/abs/path` (the one that carries UNCOMMITTED work, which a training crate living in no repo needs), and `git+https://` / `git+ssh://` with a required `#<tag|branch|sha>` (a default branch floats, and a floating ref is not a pin; a bare sha needs server-side `allowReachableSHA1InWant` and errors by name rather than falling back to a full clone). `join.libtorch: auto|cpu|cu126|cu128|rocm7.0` acquires into `~/.flodl/libtorch/` and activates it there, never the project tree (often a read-only mount on a walk-in); `auto` routes on the box's own devices, so one golden image serves NVIDIA and AMD instances. `bin:` and `source:` are mutually exclusive, loudly, and a declared `bin:` is the escape hatch that lets a borrowed box keep the last word on what it compiles. There is no toolchain field on purpose: the tree carries its own `rust-toolchain.toml` and lockfile, and `RUSTUP_TOOLCHAIN` set by fdl would silently override the operator's pin. Source that does not compile is TRANSIENT (the fix is a push away at the source; the box picks it up on its next dial), with one exception: a build that failed while the vendor's toolkit headers are demonstrably missing goes permanent, because re-dialing cannot install a package, and the error names the `apt` line.
- **`fdl publish`: the controller is the authority for what a run is, with no protocol.** It resolves a source spec into `<served>/tree` (default `~/.flodl/run/tree`), builds it once as a gate, and writes the run manifest `.fdl-run.yml` at the tree root, so one worker fetch carries source and recipe together: a worker pointed at a published tree needs nothing but the pointer, and `cwd` / `build` / `bin` / `args` come from the manifest. `args` is the sharp case and the reason this is correctness rather than ergonomics: rank children re-enter the binary with them, so a standing fleet holding its own copy trains the next run with the previous one's hyperparameters. The manifest's PRESENCE is the commit point: publish removes it before touching the tree and writes it only after the gate passes, so a box dialing mid-publish (or after a failed gate) finds none and waits, and "no manifest and no local artifact" is transient on the worker side. The build is validation, not an artifact: every worker still compiles its own (a controller producing binaries for N variants is the build matrix this design deleted), and the gate needs no GPU libtorch (`fdl libtorch download --cpu` is enough, and `--features "$FDL_GPU_FEATURE"` expands empty on a CPU variant, so one recipe line serves the gate and both worker vendors). It proves the controller's variant only: a break that exists solely under `--features rocm` still lands on a worker. Chaining runs on a standing fleet is one command: publish again, and every box picks the new run up on its next dial with nothing to edit anywhere. The report prints the worker `source.from` in BOTH spellings, labelled by the key that serves them, because rrsync re-roots every requested path under its own directory: following an absolute tree path and an `rrsync -ro` guardrail line at the same time double-roots and fails, so the recipe has to say which spelling pairs with which key.
- **Datasets route through a declared source root, and a rank never writes it.** `cluster.workers[].data_path` (and a walk-in's `join.data_path`) now reaches every rank on that host and supplies the training binary's data directory, so declaring it changes a run, not just the pre-flight checks; undeclared ships nothing and the binary keeps its own default. Underneath, `flodl::data::host_cache` is the across-run tier that owns the invariant: the source root is read-only, anything missing is acquired into the node-local cache (`~/.flodl/data`) through a caller-supplied closure (flodl never learns a URL) and published by rename (`resolve_cached`, `data_cache_dir`, `publish_atomically`); ranks read the effective root via `distributed::cluster_data_path`.
- **A data pass can be sliced into several training events**: `epoch_splits` on `DdpRunConfig` / `Trainer::builder().with_epoch_splits(n)` runs `num_epochs × n` shorter events over the same epoch permutation and LR schedule, so cadence, eval election and checkpoint boundaries land at sub-pass granularity without changing what a pass covers. Checkpoint format v5 records it (`CoverageBlock.epoch_splits`) and a resume whose slicing changed is refused up front instead of silently re-slicing coverage. `ddp-bench --epoch-splits` exposes it.
- **Mixed GPU vendors are refused at the join window when the data plane is NCCL/RCCL.** The two libraries export the same symbols and the same 128-byte unique-id format, so nothing structural rejects a mixed cohort: it hangs (or dies opaquely) inside `ncclCommInitRank` at formation, after the window deadline was spent. The hello's libtorch variant label already said the vendor, so admission now checks it: the first classifiable member seeds the cohort's vendor (the same first-member pattern as the dataset signature) and a mismatch is rejected naming both labels and both fixes (a CPU ElChe mode, which genuinely works cross-vendor and therefore disables the gate, or a one-vendor fleet). Labels that classify to no vendor gate nothing, and the check deliberately does not seed from the controller's own build: a CUDA-built coordinator-only controller in front of an all-AMD RCCL cohort is a legitimate shape.
- **Run identity, NCCL version, and arch coverage join the coherence checks**: the "arch/libtorch coherence + per-host prechecks" the membership design promised, landed where each fact actually lives. `fdl publish` stamps a fresh nonce (`run:` in `.fdl-run.yml`) every publish; it rides each worker's join hello, and the window refuses a cohort whose members hold different ids, because two boxes that fetched across a publish boundary would train two different runs (different args at minimum, and rank children re-enter the binary with them) as one world. A nonce rather than a content hash on purpose: the common re-publish is "same args, new code", which a manifest hash would call identical. The hello also carries the `(major, minor, patch)` of the NCCL/RCCL library the worker's binary actually LOADS (`ncclGetVersion` through the shim, LD_PRELOAD-aware, no CUDA context touched), and admission refuses major.minor skew under an NCCL data plane: skew refuses the handshake at formation, after the window was spent, and a walk-in fleet has no roster for `fdl probe`'s skew scan to sweep, so the window is the only place the check can live. Both are first-member-seeded consistency among joiners like the vendor gate and the dataset signature; boxes carrying no id or no version (a `--bin` box, a CPU build) gate nothing. The third check is worker-side, in `fdl join`'s prepare: the resolved libtorch's `.arch` coverage is tested against the cards this box OFFERS, so a Pascal-class box holding a cu128-only build is refused before the dial instead of dying at its first GPU op (`no kernel image is available`) after admission counted it into a quorum, scoped to the variant's own vendor and to `--devices`, and a variant without metadata gates nothing (probe owns that complaint). Controller-side per-host prechecks were deliberately NOT built: the controller cannot reach back through a NAT'd tunnel, and the worker has the filesystem. `CONTROL_PROTOCOL_VERSION` 2 → 3 (the hello grew fields and bincode is not self-describing; the bump moves a mixed-version cohort's failure to the handshake, named).
- **A mixed-model formation is refused by name instead of hanging at the first collective.** Every rank hashes the model it actually constructed (parameter and buffer names, shapes and dtypes, in declaration order, since averaging is position-addressed) and carries the signature in its control-channel handshake; the per-host relay forwards them, and the coordinator compares across the cohort before acknowledging. On a mismatch, formation aborts naming both (host, rank) pairs and the usual causes (a stale source tree, a wrong `bin:` on one box), where previously NCCL would hang on mismatched buffer sizes and CPU averaging would garble frames, after the join window was already spent. The signature is a within-cohort equality token, never persisted, deliberately not a hash of the binary (which legitimately differs per host: libtorch variant, GPU arch, rustc) nor of a source tree (only meaningful when every box built the same one). `CONTROL_PROTOCOL_VERSION` 3 → 4.
- **Walk-ins are checked for the same model signature at admission, where a mismatch costs only their own dial.** `fdl join` probes the resolved binary before the tunnel (a short CPU-only re-run: with `FLODL_INTERNAL_MODEL_SIG_PROBE` set, `Trainer::run` prints the signature and exits before auto-promote, touching no CUDA); the signature rides the join hello, and the window refuses a box building a different model the way it refuses a dataset or run-identity mismatch, so the box re-dials under `--persist` once fixed, instead of the formed cohort dying at the backstop above. The ledger's expectation is seeded by the controller's own CPU-built model (piggybacking on the schema capture it already does; its model IS the run's truth, so the very first mismatching walk-in is refused, no first-member luck), with first-member seeding as the fallback. The probe is best-effort by design: skipped (`--no-sig-probe` / `join.sig_probe: false`), failed, or timed out (a binary built against a flodl that predates the probe runs its whole main; a 120s ceiling bounds it) all join without a signature, loudly, and gate nothing. Its OUTCOME is cached across `--persist` re-dials, keyed on a digest of the probe recipe (binary path, mtime, size, the run's args), so an idle box re-dialing at the backoff cap pays the probe (or its timeout) once per actual change, not once per tick; a rebuild or a re-publish invalidates the key by construction. A binary that exits non-zero under the probe earns the loudest warning: rank children re-enter it with the same arguments after admission, so that failure usually takes the cohort's formation with it, so the probe surfaces it before the dial. Exit 0 with no signature has two very different causes — a binary older than the contract, or one that failed before reaching the trainer while exiting 0 anyway, which a read-only project directory produces on any binary that creates an output directory in `main` — so the probe names both and quotes the binary's last lines rather than guessing.
- **`docs/windows-wsl.md`** (WSL2 setup, CUDA and the honest state of AMD under WSL) with its site page, and **`rocm-capture.sh`**: a one-pass support dump of an AMD host's GPU stack (KFD topology, ROCm userspace, device permissions, both sides of the container boundary), for the box that detection cannot explain from afar.
- **`gpu_ram_share` is declarable in cluster config**: the integrated-GPU host-RAM share (the fraction of `MemTotal` an APU's aperture claims) was code-only (`TrainerConfig` / `DdpRunConfig` / `DataLoaderBuilder`), so on a cluster every host got whatever single value the binary hardcoded. It is host-hardware truth, so it travels the `data_path` road, not the manifest: per-host `cluster.workers[].gpu_ram_share`, a cluster-scope `cluster.gpu_ram_share` default (the one-line answer for a farm of identical APU boxes, legal on mixed fleets because discrete-GPU hosts ignore it), and `join.gpu_ram_share` / `--gpu-ram-share` for a walk-in. Each later road knows the box better and wins. The value fills the training binary's config only when that left the knob unset; explicit code keeps the last word, like a passed `--data-dir`. Resolution happens at ONE point (the slim-envelope build), a walk-in's own declaration rewrites it at envelope localization beside `data_path`, and ranks read it through `LocalCluster::gpu_ram_share()`. `ClusterBuilder` mirrors both scopes. Values above `1.0` stay legal in yml exactly as in code (a platform can under-state the aperture); negatives and non-numbers are refused at config load, named.
- **A standing `publish:` block in fdl.yml**: the controller's answers for `fdl publish` (source / bin / cwd / build / to / identity / args), so re-publishing a run is one bare command. Flags win field by field; a `--` tail replaces the block's `args:` outright, even empty ("explicitly none" must be sayable, since the args belong to the run). `--no-build` has no block field on purpose: a standing config that skips the gate would ship every future typo to the fleet.
- **`fdl publish --json`**: the report as JSON on stdout (notes stay on stderr): tree, served dir, built flag, toolchain, args, run id, and BOTH worker source-spec spellings keyed by the serving key they pair with. One report value feeds both renderers, so the machine twin cannot drift from the human text.
- **`fdl publish --gate <variant>`**: repeatable extra check-builds against named libtorch variants (e.g. `--gate precompiled/rocm70` on a CUDA controller), closing the "the gate proves only the controller's variant" hole: a `--features rocm`-only break now dies on the controller instead of on a worker. Each gate runs the same recipe under its own `CARGO_TARGET_DIR` (per-variant incremental caches; success is the verdict, no artifact convention). Needs no GPU; a flodl-linking crate still needs that vendor's dev headers on the controller, and a gate without them fails loudly with the exact package line.
- **`fdl join-config`, the once-per-farm wizard.** Everything the guardrail recipe asks an operator to assemble by hand, produced in one pass on the controller: the farm overlay `fdl.<label>.yml` (discovery window, `start: manual`, admission token stamped; a farm IS an env overlay, so `fdl @<label> <cmd>` targets it with the machinery that already exists), an ed25519 join key born per farm in `./.fdl/<label>/` (which gitignores itself, since it holds keys; an identity referenced from outside the farm dir draws the cross-farm-reuse warning), the composed `authorized_keys` line for the chosen door (`--door b` rrsync source pull / `a` sftp data mount / `nologin`) plus the sshd `Match` hardening block, the paste-ready worker yml in that door's dialect, a publish recipe derived from the training crate's own manifest (a path dep on flodl walks `source:` up to the dep root with `cwd:` pointing back down; a registry dep ships the crate dir alone; `--features "$FDL_GPU_FEATURE"` only when the manifest declares the vendor features; a workspace above the crate earns an explicit `bin:` caveat instead of a silent wrong guess), and a lockfile-vs-source freshness report. On an existing overlay only the `token:` line is ever touched, byte-preservingly; a user-authored overlay without one is never edited. The scaffold also wires a `commands:` entry, because a join window only opens for a command running in launcher mode and `cluster: true` is what puts it there: without one, `fdl @<label> <cmd>` resolves the base command and trains locally, silently, since training on that box is a legitimate thing to do (the entry is named after the training crate when one is readable, commented when it would be a guess, and a non-cluster command run under a discovery overlay warns for the same reason). It also guides the setup rather than only producing artifacts: a preflight names what THIS box is still missing for the chosen door before anything is written (an ssh daemon, something listening on the door port, `rrsync` for door `b` or an sftp server for door `a`, the served directory, and the two traps that produce no useful error on their own — Debian's `ssh.socket` owning the listener so `sshd_config`'s `Port` is ignored, and SELinux refusing a non-standard ssh port), each with the fix spelled in this platform's own commands (apt/dnf/brew, `ssh` vs `sshd` units, ufw vs firewalld, `semanage`). It reports which environment it inspected, because that changes what a fix MEANS: inside a container the findings describe the container and a package installed into a running one is gone after the next `--rm`, so the durable answer is the image; on the host, a project whose commands carry `docker:` builds and serves THERE, so the fix is offered in its `docker compose exec <service>` form with the services named. It writes a ready-to-install `sshd_config.d` drop-in into the farm dir and renders the whole setup as numbered steps whose commands READ those files rather than repeating their contents, ending with a door self-test that is specific to the door (every door must refuse a shell and permit the forward; only `b` must also list the served tree, only `a` must open sftp). The drop-in scopes its guardrail with `Match LocalPort`, not `Match User`: binding it to the exposed port confines every key that arrives there, including ones added later, while leaving port 22 and the operator's own logins alone — and it emits `ForceCommand` only for the tunnel-only door, since doors `a` and `b` carry their command in the key line and a daemon-level one would silently override it. Re-runs reuse credentials AND the farm's shape: door and controller are recovered from the farm's own worker yml rather than falling back to flag defaults, so reprinting the `authorized_keys` line cannot re-render the farm around it; naming a flag is how you intend a change. `--regen` rotates key and token together. `--json` is the machine twin, secrets as file paths only. The token draws from OS entropy via `getrandom`, flodl-cli's one non-serde dependency, added because an entropy binding is the dependency class you never hand-roll (the previous `/dev/urandom` read fell back to time-xor-pid SILENTLY, which on a box without the device (native Windows) meant a guessable admission token as the norm); a credential mint with no entropy now refuses loudly instead of degrading, while the publish nonce keeps its fallback (its bar is uniqueness, not secrecy).
