- **The wizard installs its own `authorized_keys` line, consent-gated.** The composed guardrail line is the artifact most likely to be mangled by hand, by exactly the audience least equipped to debug an sshd refusal. Consent is explicit only: the tty prompt or `--install-key`; `--yes` deliberately does not count (it accepts ordinary defaults, and a security-relevant mutation is not one). Only the wizard's own line is ever touched: identity is the public key material (quote-aware against `command="…"` options), foreign lines are preserved byte for byte, an existing install with different options is shown and replaced on confirm, permissions (`~/.ssh` 700, `authorized_keys` 600) are reported with the exact `chmod` and fixed on confirm, the rewrite is atomic, a symlinked `authorized_keys` is refused by name, and `/etc/ssh` is never edited. After installing, the wizard checks something is listening where workers will dial and says so if not (macOS: Remote Login hint). `--authorized-keys <path>` names a door the default cannot reach at all: an sshd in a container reads a bind-mounted key file, writable only from the host while the in-container run sees a read-only filesystem, so neither identity can install it; a host using `AuthorizedKeysFile /etc/ssh/authorized_keys.d/%u` has the same shape. Every other rule holds, `/etc/ssh` is still refused by name, and the named file's parent directory is left exactly as found, since the 0700 rule belongs to a `~/.ssh` the wizard may have to create rather than to a layout the operator already owns.
- **`fdl join-config --cloud-init`**: a user-data file embedding the worker yml, the private key and the systemd recipe (`Restart=always` + `RestartPreventExitStatus=2` + `FailureAction=poweroff`, the failure taxonomy end to end), so a cloud instance boots straight into a persistent `fdl join`. It assumes only a shell, systemd and a network: `fdl` is fetched on first boot before the unit it enables can start, and the declared door's own tooling comes with it (door `b` builds a source tree on the box, so a compiler and the transports; door `a` mounts the data root, so sshfs, whose absence prepare classes permanent, which under this very unit means exit 2 and a halt over a missing package). Every step is guarded by `command -v`, so anything baked into the image wins. A Rust toolchain installs as the service user rather than system-wide, since cargo writes its registry cache into `CARGO_HOME`, and the unit carries the matching `PATH`. `--cloud-init-user root` is wired for the images that log in as root (DigitalOcean and the AMD Developer Cloud on top of it), including root's real home at `/root`. The file states what the halt does and does not buy: where a provider bills powered-off instances, `FailureAction=poweroff` stops the work but not the meter, and only destroying the instance stops the bill. A SECRET artifact from the moment it exists; written 0600 into the farm dir, never printed.
- **`inherit-from:` grew `~` expansion, refuses remote parents by name, and is finally user-documented.** `inherit-from: ~/.flodl/farms/b300.yml` now resolves against the invoking user's home (it previously mis-resolved relative to the declaring file), which is what lets an ephemeral working directory carry a two-line farm file while the shared definition lives wherever the operator keeps it. A `://`-shaped parent is refused loudly as reserved grammar: a remote parent is config that could change under a standing fleet between two invocations, so it waits for pinning to be designed. The mechanism itself (chains, declaring-file-relative paths, cycle detection, `fdl config show` provenance) had shipped earlier with design-doc-only coverage; `docs/cli/05-manifest.md` now documents it.

### Changed

- **Formatting is rustfmt's now, enforced in CI.** Adopted for a concrete reason rather than conformity: `cargo clippy --fix` applies span-based text edits and leaves layout to rustfmt by design, so on a hand-formatted tree it produces correct code at the wrong indentation, and every mechanical fix needs a manual pass afterwards. That surfaced when correcting the MSRV unmasked 171 `collapsible_if` suggestions (the lint is MSRV-gated and had been silent while the declared floor forbade let-chains). One reformat, 385 files, and the 3026-test suite passes unchanged, which is what makes it a formatting change rather than a rewrite. Doc comments are untouched: rustfmt does not reflow comments by default. The commit is listed in `.git-blame-ignore-revs`.
- **`bincode` replaced by `bincode-next`, and supply-chain checks added.** bincode 2 is unmaintained (RUSTSEC-2025-0141) and its `3.0.0` on crates.io is a tombstone: a README plus a `lib.rs` containing only a compiler error, published because crates.io cannot archive a crate. `bincode-next` is the continuation, and the swap changes nothing observable: the same API, and a **byte-identical** wire encoding that each version decodes from the other (verified across `u32`, `String`, `Vec<i64>` and `Option<f32>`), so `CONTROL_PROTOCOL_VERSION` is unchanged and no cohort can split on it. bincode is used for the distributed wire protocol only, never for anything on disk. A new `deny` CI job runs `cargo-deny` over advisories, licenses, sources and duplicate versions; the policy and the reasoning behind each choice live in `deny.toml`, including why `MPL-2.0` is allowed for one dependency and why unmaintained advisories fire for direct dependencies only.
- **`flodl-hf`'s HuggingFace stack moved to `hf-hub` 1.0, `safetensors` 0.8 and `tokenizers` 0.23** (from 0.4 / 0.4 / 0.20). The two visible consequences are worth reading before upgrading. **Hub repo ids must be owner-qualified.** The Hub answers a bare legacy name (the pre-2024 short form, no owner segment) with a 307 to its owner-qualified home on the *metadata* endpoint; `hf-hub` 0.4's `ureq` transport followed that redirect and 1.0 does not, reporting `Repository not found` instead. `BertModel::from_pretrained("bert-base-uncased")` therefore needs `"google-bert/bert-base-uncased"`, and likewise `FacebookAI/roberta-base`, `FacebookAI/xlm-roberta-base`, `distilbert/distilbert-base-uncased`, `albert/albert-base-v2`. Owner-qualified is also the form the Hub itself now documents, and every id in this crate, its examples, its docs and its test matrix moved to it. **The MSRV consequence is not optional**: `hf-hub` 1.0 depends on `hf-xet` unconditionally (no feature gates it), which reaches `xet-core-structures` and its 1.91 floor, so the whole workspace floor moves with it. Reverting to 0.4 was the alternative considered and declined: it is the unmaintained line, and paying the floor once is cheaper than porting away from a stale Hub client later.
- **Every workspace dependency is declared once, in the root `[workspace.dependencies]`.** Members say `dep.workspace = true` and add only what is theirs. It guards one specific drift, which had already happened: two members declaring the same dependency at different versions, `flodl` asking for `flodl-hw = "0.7.0"` while `flodl-cli` asked for `"=0.7.0"`, two contracts for one crate. It deliberately does NOT guard a direct dependency drifting from a transitive one (`cargo tree -d` and cargo-deny's duplicate warnings are what catch that), and the table is full rather than only the shared entries, because a partial one needs a judgement call about which deps qualify and that judgement is itself the drift risk. **This changes the release procedure**: the internal `=X.Y.Z` pins that used to be six hardcoded companions across three member manifests are now five lines in one table, and `docs/release.md` is updated accordingly.
- **The CLI and DDP references are split into chaptered directories.** `docs/cli.md` (1568 lines) became `docs/cli/01-install.md` through `06-source-checkout.md`, and `docs/ddp.md` became `docs/ddp/01-reference.md` plus a cluster guide, an internals chapter and a troubleshooting chapter; `docs/README.md` is the new index and `docs/distributed/architecture.md` was rewritten. Both single files had passed the point where a reader could find anything in them, and the cluster surface added since 0.7.0 would have doubled them again. **Direct links to `docs/cli.md` and `docs/ddp.md` no longer resolve**; the site permalinks are unchanged, and `ci/release/03-lint-docs.sh` now validates every link, heading anchor and `/guide/` URL in the tree, which is what makes a split of this size safe to do again.
- **The `cuda-*` fdl commands are `gpu-*`**: `fdl gpu-test`, `gpu-test-all`, `gpu-test-nccl`, `gpu-test-serial`, `gpu-build`, `gpu-clippy`, `gpu-shell`. The cargo feature they pass derives from the ACTIVE libtorch variant (`cu…`/`sm…` builds `--features cuda`, `rocm…`/`gfx…` builds `--features rocm`) via `$FDL_GPU_FEATURE`, so the names say gpu because that is what they now mean. Anything calling `fdl cuda-test` (CI, scripts, muscle memory) needs the new spelling. The `ddp-bench` and `hf-ddp` vehicle manifests moved with it: `docker: gpu` + `--features "$FDL_GPU_FEATURE"` instead of a hardcoded `cuda`. The variable reaches every place a recipe can run: native (non-docker) commands get the libtorch env exactly as the compose passthrough provides it in docker mode, all six scaffolded compose services list it under `environment:` (compose passes only what is listed), and it expands empty on a CPU variant so one quoted `--features "$FDL_GPU_FEATURE"` line serves a CPU gate and both worker vendors.
- **The GPU API is vendor-neutral**: `cuda_*` to `gpu_*`, `Cuda*` to `Gpu*`, C ABI `flodl_cuda_*` to `flodl_gpu_*`. Deprecated aliases for all 33 renamed items ship in `flodl::compat`, re-exported from the crate root and from `tensor`/`nn`, so existing paths keep resolving. `flodl_nccl_*` (RCCL exports the nccl names verbatim), `set_cudnn_benchmark` (routed to MIOpen on ROCm) and `cuda_compute_capability` (an NVIDIA-only concept, and an error on ROCm) keep their names.
- **Feature flags name the axis they select**: `gpu` is the vendor-neutral gate, `cuda` and `rocm` select a backend and imply it. `--features cuda` behaves as before. `build.rs` rejects both vendors at once and `gpu` without one.
- **`detect_gpus()` returns only devices the running build can address.** `detect_gpus_physical()` ignores the vendor filter and answers the provisioning question (which libtorch variant covers this box).
- **Integrated GPUs no longer double-count memory.** An APU's aperture is carved out of host RAM, so the data plane reserves it once, subtracting unrealized headroom only. `gpu_ram_share` on `DataLoaderBuilder`, `TrainerConfig` and `DdpRunConfig` overrides the fraction, and is required on a multi-socket APU, where each package carries its own aperture while `/proc/meminfo` is system-wide and the kernel exposes no per-package equivalent to correct with. That case is refused as an ordinary error from `DataLoaderBuilder::build` / rank-worker construction, not from inside the per-epoch arithmetic: every input to the verdict (the knob, the socket count, whether the device is integrated) is static for the process, so it is decided once where a `Result` already exists. The gate counts CPU packages, not NUMA nodes: NPS and Sub-NUMA Clustering split one socket's memory into several nodes without multiplying the aperture, so a single-socket machine keeps sizing itself whatever its NUMA layout.
- **The system ROCm runtime precedes libtorch's bundled copy on `LD_LIBRARY_PATH`** in cluster pre-build, remote dispatch, the container images, and the native-build recipe `fdl setup` prints (which honors `$ROCM_PATH`, defaulting to `/opt/rocm`). libtorch-rocm bundles the whole userspace ROCm stack, and letting it win over the host's segfaults at the first GPU op, so a recipe printed the other way round is that configuration handed to the user to paste.
- **`fdl diagnose` reports GPUs by vendor and prints the sweep's findings.** The section was headed CUDA and said "No CUDA devices available" on a box whose cards are AMD; an AMD GPU present with no ROCm runtime installed, a device the sweep deliberately does not count, now surfaces there as well as in `fdl probe`.
- **Lint gates deny instead of warn.** `-W clippy::all` is a no-op, since `clippy::all` is warn-by-default: every clippy step in CI, in `fdl clippy` / `fdl gpu-clippy`, and in the `fdl init` scaffold could only fail on a compile error the build already covered. They now pass `-D warnings` (`-D clippy::all` in the scaffold, so a half-written function does not block the lint run), and clippy runs ahead of the build in all three CI jobs, which costs nothing because the two share dependency rlibs and compile the workspace twice whichever order they run in.

- **The pinned consensus decode is now RAM-neutral: the reply decodes back into the snapshot staging the rank just streamed from, instead of a second locked model copy.** The 0.7.0 pinned decode bought a truly-async H2D writeback at the cost of one page-locked f32 model copy per rank, measured on a RAM-tight host as a net regression (locked pages are unswappable, so the kernel evicted the per-window working set instead: +6–8% epoch wall, all of it swap thrash inside the reduce windows), which the MemAvailable gate could only answer by giving that host up entirely. The observation that removes the trade-off: the reply's frame mirrors the schema of the snapshot the rank just sent, and the snapshot staging's bytes are dead the moment the streamed encode has read them, so the client now accepts the request tensors themselves as decode destinations (`CpuReduceClient::set_decode_into_request` / `arm_decode_into`), and the consensus lands in the same pinned storage `load_averaged` then reads, under the same single-consumer fence chain the staging already carried. Zero marginal locked memory, no affordability gate: RAM-tight hosts get the async writeback back instead of the fresh-alloc fallback (rig-verified at the motivating A/B's anchor invocation: epoch wall 289.2s vs 290.2–292.8s baseline and 308.5–315.9s for the gated predecessor's ungated arm, with swap growth collapsing from +2.0GB to +0.8MB). Two hazards this surfaced are guarded: a zero-mass round's reply (meaningless zeros the caller keep-locals over) must not clobber the very tensors keep-local returns (the realized-work weight rides the wire ahead of the payloads precisely so the decode can fall back to fresh allocs before touching the staging), and a degenerate snapshot fallback shipping non-CPU or dtype-mismatched tensors is refused per payload rather than written through. Under `bf16_wire` the reply lands VERBATIM in the bf16 snapshot staging (no information loss: the controller folds in f32 and the wire is bf16 either way) and the writeback stages through a flat device-side bf16 bounce, because libtorch's `copy_` casts on the *source* device for cross-device + cross-dtype copies: a direct `f32-param ← bf16-pinned` copy would materialize a pageable f32 temp on the host and silently degrade to a synchronous bounce. Net effect for bf16 cohorts: half the writeback H2D bytes on top of the zero-locked-RAM property; the weight-divergence triple keeps full f32 precision via a per-tensor path (never a model-sized upcast transient). The slot-based decode staging, its `DECODE_SLOT_*` tags, and the gate's decode role are retired (`pinned_decode_affordable` stays, documented as historical: the reasoning applies to the snapshot staging a future RAM-tight host may need to interrogate).
- **Structural zeros never cross the cluster wire anymore: a payload can declare itself all-zeros (`nbytes = 0`, schema intact) and every tier folds it without materializing it.** The 0.7.0 formation-broadcast fix stopped *materializing* the non-root zeros model (~762MB of RAM per rank at 190M params) but still *shipped* the zero bytes: every non-root rank pushed a full model of zeros up the wire, HMAC-SHA256'd on both ends, decoded and accumulated by the relay fold: pure overhead scaling with model size × (N−1) ranks, at formation time on every multi-host run, and worst exactly where the CPU plane aims (slow inter-node links). Three senders know their payload is structurally zeros without scanning anything, and all three now declare it instead of shipping it: the formation broadcast's non-root ranks, an idle rank's zero-mass window contribution (γ-mass 0 fused as scale-by-zero, a full model of ±0.0 per idle window before), and the controller's degenerate zero-mass scatter. Elision is a member of the fold monoid: zeros + zeros = zeros, so an all-elided round stays elided through the relay fold (seed→sums promotion is now lazy, firing only at the first non-elided deposit), through `sum_frames`, and back out the scatter: it composes through fold tiers the same way the mass algebra does, hierarchical-relay-ready. The fold reads elided as "adds nothing", which is IEEE-exact, with one deliberate semantic improvement: a NaN-poisoned idle rank can no longer forward `0.0 × NaN = NaN` into the consensus, because an elided contribution is *true* zeros. Receivers decode elided payloads to zeros of the declared shape (covering the zero-mass scatter), and the checkpoint forge skips zero-mass rounds the same way ranks do, since the consensus checkpoint is a consensus consumer too. `PROTOCOL_VERSION` 2 → 3: a v2 reader would reject an elided frame loudly anyway (schema error at the fold), but the bump moves a mixed-version cohort's failure to the handshake (first contact, named cause) instead of the first idle window.

### Fixed

- **The declared minimum Rust version was wrong, and nothing checked it.** `rust-version` said `1.85` while the source has used let-chains since before 0.7.0, which stabilised in **1.88**; the transitive `icu_*` crates independently require 1.86. Cargo trusts that field, so a user on 1.85 got past the version check and then hit `error[E0658]: let expressions in this position are unstable` in someone else's crate rather than a refusal naming the version they needed. It is now **1.91**, measured rather than assumed, and three floors stack to get there: 1.88 is our own source's (let-chains), `bincode-next` raises it to 1.90, and `xet-core-structures` to 1.91. A new `msrv` CI job compiles the workspace on exactly the toolchain `Cargo.toml` declares, reading the value from the manifest so the two cannot drift, and it is what caught the third one: `xet-core-structures` arrives through hf-hub 1.0's mandatory `hf-xet` dependency, declares no `rust-version` of its own, and calls `str::floor_char_boundary`, so cargo could neither warn nor resolve around it, and MSRV-aware resolution had nothing to act on. It simply failed to compile, in a transitive crate, on the declared floor. Note this floor tracks the ecosystem for the same reason: with no committed lockfile, consumers resolve fresh, so a transitive dependency raising its own `rust-version` raises this one.
- **A native project built on macOS and then refused to start.** `fdl` composed `LD_LIBRARY_PATH` for native commands, which macOS's loader ignores, so a scaffolded project linked and then died at launch with `dyld: Library not loaded: @rpath/libtorch.dylib`. Exporting `DYLD_LIBRARY_PATH` beside it does not fix this: run lines execute through `sh -c`, `/bin/sh` is SIP restricted, and dyld purges every `DYLD_*` from a restricted process before the shell execs anything. An rpath from `flodl-sys`'s build script does not reach the consuming binary either, since `cargo:rustc-link-arg` does not propagate to dependents. So macOS native commands now pass `RUSTFLAGS=-C link-arg=-Wl,-rpath,<variant>/lib`, which SIP does not touch and which applies to the final link: the binary locates libtorch itself, and keeps working when launched directly rather than through `fdl run`. Filled only when absent, so a caller's own `RUSTFLAGS` still wins, and Linux is untouched.

  Behind that sat a second layer, which the first fix exposed by getting far enough to reach it: upstream's `libtorch-macos-arm64` archive BUNDLES `lib/libomp.dylib` and then has `libtorch_cpu.dylib` depend on it by absolute Homebrew path, so the load failed on any box without that formula while the library it wanted sat in the same directory as the dylib asking for it. `brew install libomp` is the wrong answer there: it installs a second, possibly ABI-divergent copy of something already present. `fdl libtorch download` (and `fdl setup` through it) now repoints those dependencies at `@loader_path/libomp.dylib` after extracting, and re-signs, since editing a Mach-O invalidates its signature and arm64 will not load an unsigned one. `@loader_path` rather than `@rpath` because the bundled copy is a sibling of every dylib referencing it, so it resolves without depending on the referrer carrying a correct `LC_RPATH`. The three tools this needs are checked BEFORE anything is modified and the step is advisory, so a box without the command line tools is told to run `xcode-select --install` rather than left holding a patched library it cannot re-sign.
- **The bootstrap's source fallback could not run where it was most needed.** `./fdl` verifies a downloaded release binary by running it, and replaces it from source when it cannot run — which is exactly what happens on a distribution whose glibc is older than the build base. But the fallback did `cargo build -p flodl-cli` in the script's own directory, so it only worked inside a checkout: in the `curl`-and-run shape, in a scaffolded project, or on a cloud instance that fetched the script and nothing else, it failed with `could not find Cargo.toml`. It now installs from crates.io when there is no local workspace, which needs nothing but cargo. Verified end to end on a RHEL 9 userland, where the released binary cannot run at all: bare directory, one script, working `fdl` seven seconds later.
- **The released `fdl` binaries were built on a floating base.** `release-cli.yml` used `ubuntu-latest` for the Linux targets, and a dynamically linked binary cannot run where glibc is older than the one it was built against — so the base is a floor on who can use the release, and that floor moved whenever GitHub rotated the image, with no code change and no release note. The Linux runners are pinned, and a gate now reads the artifact's own maximum `GLIBC_` requirement and fails the build if it rises above the declared floor: raising it drops users, so it should be a decision rather than a side effect.
- **Every `fdl init` scaffold since crates.io tightened its API policy carried a floating git dependency.** The scaffold prefers the latest published flodl version, but its crates.io query sent no identifying `User-Agent`, which crates.io's data-access policy rejects, so the "registry unavailable" fallback (`flodl = { git = … }`, default branch, floating) had silently become the norm on every box, and `fdl add flodl-hf` then refused the very scaffold `fdl init` produced ("needs a pinnable crates.io version"). Found by finally running the stranger's path end to end: a throwaway crate on a box that never built flodl. The query now goes through the update check's probe (correct `User-Agent`, `--fail`, 2s cap), and the offline fallback pins fdl's own version (fdl and flodl are workspace-versioned twins), so the scaffold is always a pinnable registry dep, never a git one.
- **The native scaffold's printed next steps did not work.** `./fdl libtorch download --cpu` then `./fdl build` failed at `flodl-sys`'s build script: fdl downloaded libtorch but never exported `LIBTORCH_PATH` for native commands (docker services get it from their compose env; native got a comment telling the user to export it by hand), and a successful build then failed at `./fdl run` with `libtorch_cpu.so: cannot open shared object file` for the same reason on the `LD_LIBRARY_PATH` side. Native commands now fill both from the active variant (inherited values kept as the override), so download, build and run work with zero hand exports, on the registry crate, verified end to end on two boxes (one with a cold rustup).
- **`fdl libtorch download` built an invalid URL on Windows.** Windows archives carry a `-win-` infix that the Linux ones do not, and nothing had ever evaluated that arm on a Windows host, so the 404 shipped unnoticed.
- **`fdl setup` fetched an x86_64 libtorch on Apple Silicon.** A Docker-mounted project needs the container's libtorch, which upstream publishes for Linux x86_64 but not for Linux aarch64. Intel Macs still force the Linux build; Apple Silicon gets the host build and a pointer to the wheel-extraction steps, instead of one that cannot load in a linux/arm64 container.
- **A GPU could be reported as covered by a libtorch variant that ships no kernel for it.** The compute-capability check substring-searched the raw `archs=` list, so a bare major matched the minor of an unrelated entry: a Maxwell `sm_50`/`sm_52` card tested its `5` against cu128's `7.0 7.5 …`, hit the `5` inside `7.5`, and `fdl diagnose` printed OK for a build whose first kernel launch fails with `no kernel image is available for execution on the device`. The list is tokenized before matching now. A `+PTX` suffix on a list entry is understood.
- **`fdl setup` could finish with no `libtorch/.active`.** The CPU download deliberately does not claim the pointer so a GPU variant fetched after it can, but on a host where no GPU variant follows, nothing ever wrote it, leaving `fdl diagnose` reporting "no active variant" immediately after a successful setup. CPU now claims the pointer if it is still unclaimed.

- **A record-path collision no longer drops a rank out of the monitor tree**: `build_tree` descends by path segment, and a node claimed as a *leaf* by one producer and as an *interior* by another (two producers disagreeing on tiering, which `cohort_tiering` exists to prevent) had its terminal entry filtered out of the descent while the interior branch was taken anyway. In debug a `debug_assert!` named it; in **release** that rank's numbers simply vanished from the tree, with no warning and with the parent's roll-up averaging over the survivors as if the missing rank had never reported: a wrong number presented with full confidence, the same failure class as the absent-is-not-zero defects above. The collision now warns once and the stray's mass folds into its parent's aggregate, so the work-weighted numbers stay whole. It deliberately gets **no record of its own**: a record's `path` *is* its file path in the record log, so emitting it as a child of its own parent would append its lines into the parent's file, so the anomaly is surfaced as a warning rather than papered over with a synthetic path segment that would materialize a real file. Promoting the assert to a release panic was rejected for the reason the record log already swallows its I/O errors: a monitoring artifact must never be able to kill a training run. The sibling case that carried no assert at all is covered too: two leaves claiming *one* path (the "metrics feed splits in half" hazard named on `cohort_tiering`) now warn instead of silently first-winning.
- **`--record-log` says where it actually writes**: a relative path resolves against the *launcher's* cwd rather than the directory it was typed in, which is how `ddp-bench/runs/x` silently becomes `ddp-bench/ddp-bench/runs/x`; nothing echoed the destination, so the doubling was found by hunting for the files. The launcher now prints the resolved directory at startup. It joins the cwd rather than canonicalizing, because the directory is created lazily at the first record and canonicalizing a path that does not exist yet fails exactly when the message is most wanted.

## [0.7.0] - 2026-07-29

> Upgrading from 0.6.0? Additive almost everywhere. The only compile-time
> breaks are two shapes in `flodl::monitor::record` (`Res` gained two
> public fields, and `to_record_json` / `flat_records` take `Option<u64>`
> for `tick`), plus a fifth argument on
> `distributed::launcher::run_launcher_with_config` for direct callers.
> Nothing to do if you do not name those. See
> [UPGRADE.md](UPGRADE.md) for the step-by-step migration.

### Changed (breaking)

- **`distributed::launcher::run_launcher_with_config` takes a fifth argument**, the `Option<[u8; 32]>` model signature that seeds the join window's coherence check (pass `None` to keep first-member seeding). A compile-time error for direct callers; `Trainer::run` / `Trainer::builder().run()` users are unaffected — the trampoline supplies it.

Beyond that, two public items in `flodl::monitor::record` changed shape. Both
are consequences of the interval/cadence work described under **Added** below,
and both are compile-time errors rather than silent behavior changes. Nothing
else in the crate's public surface was removed or re-signatured.

- **`Res` gained two public fields and is not `#[non_exhaustive]`.** The
  resource record grew `gpu_util_max` and `vram_alloc_max: Option<f64>` so a
  point can summarise an *interval* rather than an instant (see the envelope
  entry below for why the previous latest-wins reading reported a busy GPU as
  idle). Reading fields is unaffected; **constructing `Res` with a struct
  literal now fails to compile** until the two fields are supplied. Migration is
  `Res { gpu_util, vram_alloc, vram_total, ..Default::default() }`, which is
  also future-proof against further envelope fields.
- **`NodeRecord::to_record_json` and `NodeRecord::flat_records` take
  `Option<u64>` for `tick`** (was `u64`). An epoch record closes an epoch rather
  than belonging to a sub-epoch window, so it genuinely has no window index, and
  `0` would have been indistinguishable from the first window. Callers pass
  `Some(tick)` for a window record and `None` for an epoch record.

### Added

- **Resource curves carry the interval, not the last reading** — every resource point on the dashboard (and in epoch records) now summarizes the min/mean/max **envelope** of all samples taken since the previous report, instead of whichever ~500ms reading happened to be latest at publication time — which, publication riding the reduce boundary, was usually mid-sync and therefore idle: a GPU busy for most of an interval rendered as quiet. `Res` gains `gpu_util_max` / `vram_alloc_max`, with reductions chosen per meaning: a parent's utilization **peak** takes the Max over children (averaging peaks makes one pegged rank beside three idle ones read quarter-busy), while VRAM peaks **Sum** (sum-of-peaks is the conservative "how much VRAM the cohort needed available"; peak-of-sum would understate it). The wire and record shapes stay flat. A window that saw no fresh sample still reports nothing — the accumulator being empty *is* the absent≠zero guarantee, replacing the explicit freshness flag. New public `flodl::monitor::envelope` (`Envelope`, `EnvelopeAcc`) — deliberately generic, because the same min/mean/max shape is next wanted by `/history` decimation and the graph timing heat map. Rig-verified on a 20-epoch cluster run: 195 of 306 GPU-carrying records show a peak more than a point above the mean, with the early spread (mean 19.3 vs peak 64.1) narrowing late — startup and sync-dominated time, which is itself signal the single sample was hiding.
- **`[worker-mem]` teardown line: each rank reports what its resident set is actually made of** — alongside the existing `[worker-prof]` split (same `-vv` gate), every rank now prints one `/proc/self/smaps_rollup` read at teardown: `rss` / `pss` / `private` / `shared`. RSS double-counts pages physically shared between co-hosted ranks (libtorch `.so` text, CUDA driver mappings — one copy per host however many ranks map them), so per-host budget arithmetic built on `N × rank RSS` overstates the bill by roughly `rss − pss` per additional co-hosted rank. PSS and the private/shared split are the numbers that arithmetic actually needs; until now nothing reported them, and the gap was estimated (~560MB/rank on the reference rig) rather than measured. Off Linux, or on any parse surprise, the line is simply absent — it is a diagnostic and never affects teardown.
- **Dashboard: children are linked under the breadcrumb, and `sweep.sh` saves every cell's portal archive** — each level's children render as links beside the breadcrumb so a cluster run is browsable without going through the compare table, and the bench sweep passes `--save-dashboard` per cell so a full sweep leaves one self-contained portal archive per (model, mode) alongside the timeline artifacts.

- **Cluster ranks get the two-stage reader ring — the network-storage pipeline the solo loader already had** — the solo streaming `DataLoader` runs a reader thread that fetches batches into a bounded pageable-RAM ring while a second thread runs the device transfers, lifting the delivery ceiling from `1/(t_read + t_transfer)` to `1/max(t_read, t_transfer)` and absorbing read jitter; the cluster path had fetch and transfer serialized on one thread, so a slow storage read stalled the batch outright. Clusters being precisely where remote storage lives (`/flodl/data` over NAS / virtiofs / S3-FUSE), the asymmetry was exactly backwards — and it bit hardest in the *simplest* configuration: the stager (the cluster path's read-ahead into pinned RAM) is advisory-driven and advisories only exist on the progressive modes, so plain sync-mode cluster runs had no read-ahead of any kind. The distributed prefetch worker now spawns the same reader-and-ring in front of its transfer stage, fed by a pump that forwards each chunk's queued batches so the reader genuinely runs ahead rather than lockstepping one fetch behind (verified structurally: a stalled consumer bounds the single-stage shape at 2 fetched batches, the ring shape reads on — a test pins both bounds with a slow-storage dataset). Batch order, the transient-OOM patience, mid-epoch `ExtendPartition` submissions, and the teardown latch all carry over; the ring is sized per plan boundary from live `MemAvailable` under the same `ram_max_usage` knob the stager budgets from, capped at the flow-buffer depth (a retained tier always sits beside it, so the ring absorbs jitter rather than claiming capacity), and `--ram-max-usage 0` keeps the single-stage shape as the explicit kill-switch, matching solo semantics. The `-vv` sizing line grows a `ring=N` field. On the rig's staged-RAM workloads this changes little by design — its case is a stager miss or a dormant stager against genuinely remote storage, where the read otherwise lands inline on the delivery thread.
- **`fdl init` scaffolds `.env.example`, and the committed-template convention is now written down** — the generated `docker-compose.yml` reads `.env` and the generated `.gitignore` ignores it, so a scaffolded project was handing you the knob and the ignore rule with nothing saying what belongs in the file. Docker modes now get a `.env.example` listing exactly the variables *that mode's* compose substitutes and no others: mounted mode documents the libtorch variant overrides and CUDA image version/tag alongside `UID`/`GID` and `CARGO_BUILD_JOBS`, baked mode documents only the three that apply (verified by generating all three modes and diffing the documented names against the compose file's substitutions in both directions). Native mode generates no compose, so it gets no template. The generated compose also forwards `FLODL_VERBOSITY` and `FLODL_NET_TIMEOUT_SCALE` on both `dev` and `cuda`, which closes a real gap rather than just documenting one: `fdl -v/-vv` sets the verbosity in its own environment, but compose only passes through variables it names, so **verbosity never reached a scaffolded project's container**. `CUDA_VERSION`/`CUDA_TAG` are documented with the caveat that they bite only for direct `docker compose` calls, since `fdl` derives both from the active libtorch variant's `.arch` and overrides whatever `.env` says — the same trap `LIBTORCH_CPU_PATH` carries. `CONTRIBUTING.md` now names the convention once — `*.example` committed, your copy gitignored, made by copying — as a table covering both `fdl.yml.example` and `.env.example`, with the reason `UID`/`GID` matters (compose defaults to `1000:1000`, right on most Linux boxes, wrong on macOS at `501:20`). README's "choose your path" table gains the row it was missing entirely, for people forking or hacking on flodl rather than consuming it as a crate.
- **Apple Silicon (Mac M-series) support path, documented end to end** — new `docs/mac-apple-silicon.md` (thanks @newQuery) covers the route flodl actually runs on a Mac: CPU through the Docker `dev` service on Linux arm64, since CUDA does not exist on macOS. It carries the parts you only learn by doing it — extracting a Linux arm64 libtorch from the PyPI wheel (PyTorch publishes no `libtorch-linux-aarch64.zip`), why re-pointing `libtorch/precompiled/cpu` with a symlink is the fix rather than `LIBTORCH_CPU_PATH` (`fdl` sets that variable itself, so it overrides whatever `.env` says), macOS UID/GID being 501/20 rather than 1000/1000, and the spurious `ld: cannot find -ltorch` that appears when the linker runs more parallel lookups against the virtiofs-mounted `libtorch_cpu.so` than the mount can serve. `docker-compose.yml` now passes `CARGO_BUILD_JOBS` through to the `dev` service so that last one is fixable from `.env`, and a new committed `.env.example` documents every compose knob (host UID/GID, libtorch variant overrides, CUDA tag, the job throttle, verbosity). `fdl init` emits the same passthrough and gitignores `.env` in both baked and mounted modes, so a scaffolded project inherits the fix instead of needing the troubleshooting section to find it.
- **A new CLI flag now shows up in `fdl <cmd> -h` on its own** — the cached `--fdl-schema` surface was invalidated against the command's `fdl.yml` and nothing else, but the schema of a `compile: true` cargo entry is *compiled from the crate's sources*. So adding a flag left the cache stale with **no signal at all**: help kept rendering the previous surface, indefinitely, while the binary itself was correct — the confusing direction, since `--fdl-schema` on the binary showed the new flag and only `-h` disagreed. The fix is to compare the cache against every file whose edit could change the schema: the config *and*, for a compiling command, every `.rs` and `Cargo.toml` under the command directory. Deliberately coarse rather than an attempt to locate the files that *define* the schema — watching too much costs one extra probe, exactly what editing the yml already costs, while watching too little is the bug being fixed, and a precise scan would reintroduce it the moment a `#[derive(FdlArgs)]` struct referenced a constant from another module. It is cheap enough to sit in front of `-h` (23 files in ~0.1 ms for `ddp-bench`, against a probe that spins a container and runs cargo), skips `target/`, and is bounded so a pathological tree degrades to config-only invalidation rather than stalling help. Dependency crates are **not** followed: a command's schema comes from its own CLI struct, and following its dependencies would invalidate on every edit anywhere in the workspace — recompiling on nearly every `-h`, which is the cost the cache exists to avoid. Verified end to end on `ddp-bench`: an unchanged tree still serves the cache in 0.10 s with no probe, a flag added without touching the yml appears in `-h`, and removing it makes it disappear again.
- **The dashboard has a light theme, and follows your desktop by default** — a toggle at the top right of the header, remembered per browser, and unset the page reads `prefers-color-scheme`: a dark desktop gets the dark dashboard (the better place to watch a long run), a light one gets light without having to ask. The palette is lifted verbatim from flodl.dev, which already declared it under the same variable names, so the dashboard and the site are one product rather than two takes on a similar idea. Three parts of this were not CSS. **Canvas has no cascade**, so the chart chrome (axes, grid, reference lines) was hardcoded and now resolves from the stylesheet once per render — the CSS stays the single source of truth and a toggle forces a redraw, since a canvas holds no restyleable state. **The series palette swaps**: the dark ramp ends in pale tints that are unreadable on white, so light gets twelve darker values anchored on the site's light accents, in the same order so a series keeps its colour. And the theme resolves in a `<script>` in `<head>` **before the body paints**, because reading the preference after first paint is what makes a page flash the wrong theme on every load.
  - **The saved dashboard is the publication case.** An archive follows the reader's OS like the live page, but `Trainer::builder().dashboard_theme("light")` (ddp-bench: `--dashboard-theme light`) pins it, because a figure in a paper should not change appearance with the reviewer's desktop. Nothing is locked in at training time: every saved page carries the choice as one line near the top (`const ARCHIVE_THEME=null;`), so re-theming an artifact you already have is a single edit — and the reader's own toggle still overrides a pinned default, since pinning sets the default rather than removing the control. An unrecognized theme warns loudly and leaves the choice to the reader instead of theming the page at random.
  - The page now declares **two** `<script>` blocks, the theme init first because it reads the injected `ARCHIVE_THEME`. The test that pinned "exactly one block" existed to guarantee the injectors' constants land ahead of the code that reads them, so it was replaced by that actual invariant — *the first block must be the theme init* — which is a stronger assertion than a count, and the element/handler sweep now walks every block rather than assuming one.
- **The dashboard is now savable as one file, and the saved file is the whole portal** — `Trainer::builder().save_dashboard(path)` (ddp-bench: `--save-dashboard`, writing `<run_dir>/dashboard.html` beside `timeline.html`) writes the run's dashboard as a single self-contained HTML artifact: every level browsable, both metric cadences interleaved, `#path=` deep links working, the model graph SVG and the hyperparameters inline. No server, no sibling files, nothing fetched. `save_html` already existed but baked only the *epoch feed*, so a saved cluster run rendered the fallback tree rather than the portal it had been showing live; the archive now carries the record plane itself (`meta` declarations first, then every retained record). Three properties make it safe to leave on: it **needs no dashboard port** (persisting a dashboard should not require serving one), it **cannot grow without bound** (the record plane is a ring, so a longer run shortens the artifact's *horizon* instead of enlarging its *file* — what keeps it attachable to a ticket), and it is written **at teardown after the record stream drains**, so it captures the end of the run. Rig-verified on the 3-rank heterogeneous cluster: 168 KB carrying 169 records across all six paths, a 37.6 KB graph SVG, the merged hyperparameter/parameter-count block, and the host-attributed hardware inventory.
  - **Ownership fix that made the headless case possible at all.** The record plane lived *inside* the HTTP server, so `records_snapshot` could only see it when a port was bound — `--save-dashboard` without `--monitor` produced a page with `ARCHIVE_RECORDS=[]`, a persisted dashboard silently requiring a live one. The store is now owned by the `Monitor` (shared with the server when one binds, via `DashboardServer::start_with_records`), because the record plane is a data structure and not an HTTP concern. `push_records` inserts locally **only** when headless: with a server bound its handler already inserts into that same store, and doing both would double every row.
  - **Two more places gated the artifact on the live page.** The run-scoped cards (hyperparameters + graph SVG) were installed only when a monitor port was set, so a headless archive had neither — they belong to the artifact as much as to the page. And on the cluster path the rank's whole dashboard-setup emission was wrapped in `if port.is_some()`, even though it internally port-gates only the one frame that needs a port (`DashboardRegister`) and ships the SVG / metadata / hardware unconditionally — so a rank stashed its graph and never sent it. All three defects presented identically: a plausible-looking file containing nothing, rather than an error.
  - **Archive-before-shutdown was losing the tail.** `finish_inner` built the archive *before* shutting the server down, but `push_records` crosses a channel and `shutdown` is what drains it and joins the handler — so the archive silently dropped however much of the stream was still in flight, i.e. the end of the run, which is the part it is most wanted for. Both paths now shut down first.
  - The saved page enters record mode **directly** rather than through the live latch, which resets a deep-linked viewer to root: every level is baked in an archive, so `#path=` has to keep working. The epoch feed still contributes only the run clock and host gauges, exactly as it does live.
- **User scalars can declare how they roll up across ranks — `Trainer::builder().scalar_reduction(key, Reduction)`** — and until now they could not, which was a live wrong answer rather than a missing feature. Framework metrics have authoritative reductions (`loss` is a work-weighted mean, `throughput` sums, `data_starve` takes the worst rank), but a user scalar cannot be guessed, so it defaults to `Mean` — right for a rate or an accuracy, wrong for a **count** (`tokens_seen`) or an **extremum** (`peak_mem_gb`). The reduction vocabulary and the `meta` declaration record both already existed and were plumbed end to end, but **nothing ever populated the map and nothing ever emitted the record**, so every non-core metric was averaged across ranks and the portal's legend *asserted* it — rendering `tokens_seen (mean)` with full confidence. Declarations now reach every consumer automatically by riding the stream's `meta` record, which each subscriber receives ahead of any data record, so a viewer can never interpret a metric without knowing how it rolled up. Core keys ignore any declaration. This is the cheap half of the long-deferred inline-override API: a builder-level map is **controller-side**, so it needs no wire change — only the per-call `record_scalar(.., Reduction)` form breaks the wire, and that stays deferred.
  - `set_metadata` used to *replace* the blob that `watch` merged parameter counts into, so the natural reading order — `watch(&model)` then `set_metadata(cfg)` — silently dropped `total`/`trainable`/`frozen`. The in-repo archive test used exactly that order while asserting only on the config keys, which is why it went unnoticed. Both writers now funnel through one publisher: call order cannot matter, and your own keys still win on collision.
- **The live dashboard is now a portal: one view, repeated at every level of the run** — the page renders from the record plane's paths instead of a fixed set of per-device tabs, so `root` → host → rank is something you *browse*. Every level draws the same way (its own metrics plotted, its resources plotted, its children compared and clickable, its log listing epoch and sub-epoch rows **interleaved** on the one axis the two cadences share), a breadcrumb replaces the tab bar, and each level is linkable (`#path=root/flodl-pascal/rank1`) with the browser's Back button walking back up. Drilling in re-subscribes to the child path, so the page watches **one level** rather than the whole cluster: a 300-rank run costs the same to watch as a 3-rank one, which is the point of the depth-1 scoping the record plane was built with. Two things the levels needed to be readable: the legend is **level-aware** — at an interior node every metric is a work-weighted roll-up over the direct children, so it names the reduction (`loss (mean)`, `throughput (sum)`), while at a leaf it is a raw measurement and stays the bare key, and children read `rank1 · GTX 1060 6GB` from the per-node label — and `work` is a **column, never a curve**, because its unit differs by cadence (steps for a window, batch share for an epoch), so plotting both as one series would compare different units. Alerts get their own feed with the origin path as a link straight to the rank that raised it, fed by a subscription pinned at `root` (where alert scope is the whole subtree) so a rank loss in an unwatched branch still reaches you whatever level you are on. `/events` keeps exactly one job it alone can do — the run clock (epoch/total, ETA, elapsed, completion) and the host CPU/RAM gauges, which are per-host facts deliberately outside the record tree and so are shown as gauges rather than as a curve on an axis they do not share. A run with **no** record plane (any single-process run, and the baked `save_html` archive) has no levels to browse, so the page builds them from that same epoch feed: the run level, plus one child per device when the run reports two or more — mirroring the record plane's own tiering rule, where a lone device collapses into root rather than adding a level that repeats it. So the per-GPU tabs' four curves (VRAM, utilization, throughput, batch share) survive as a per-device *level*, and one renderer covers live cluster, live single-process, and archive alike.
  - **Fixes a duplicate-row defect the rewrite inherited.** `/events` replays its whole epoch history on every connect and `EventSource` reconnects on its own after any drop, so keying fallback rows on a cumulative clock appended every epoch again — and pushed each later epoch further along a clock that only grows. Rows now key on the epoch number, verified idempotent across a full replay followed by a live epoch.
  - The two `<script>` injectors (`serve_html`, `build_archive`) now `replacen(.., 1)` rather than `replace`, which prepended their constants to *every* script block and was invisible only while the template happened to have exactly one. Pinned by a test, along with the page's element-id and inline-handler references and the archive's field-name contract — a typo'd id or a renamed serializer field fails **silently** in a browser, so the string-checkable half is checked in Rust.
  - **The run-scoped cards are back at root, and the parameter counts now reach a cluster dashboard at all.** The graph architecture SVG and the "Training Configuration" card describe the whole run rather than a level, so the portal shows them at `root` only — but nothing was feeding them on a cluster run. Two causes, both producer-side: `capture_param_info` pushed the merged blob straight to `self.server`, which is `None` on the launcher (there `serve()` returns early and the `ClusterDashboardSink` owns the real server), so the `total` / `trainable` / `frozen` counts were dropped on exactly the runs with the most to report; and `set_metadata` *replaced* the blob that `watch` had merged into, so the natural reading order — `watch(&model)` then `set_metadata(cfg)` — silently discarded the counts. Both writers now funnel through one cluster-aware publisher: call order no longer matters, and your own keys still win on collision. Pinned by tests over both orders.
  - **A deep model's graph no longer renders as a mile-high ribbon.** Graphviz lays ResNet-56 out at roughly 272 × 7500 points, and the card's `width:100%` upscaled that thinness to the card width, multiplying the height by the same factor into tens of thousands of pixels. The SVG now renders at natural size (shrinking only when genuinely wider than the card) and the opened card caps at 70% of the viewport and scrolls internally, so drilling into a deep model no longer pushes every other card off the page.
  - Validated against the real 3-rank heterogeneous rig stream (72 records, 6 levels): the tree resolves to the actual cohort, every level carries both cadences interleaved, replay and reversed arrival (the history-preamble-after-live case) produce identical series, a `root`-scoped subscription alone fills the children view without leaking a grandchild, and per-device work scales differ by cadence as documented. The rendered page itself is not machine-checkable here — the assertions cover the level logic, not the pixels.
- **The per-epoch feed now emits records too, interleaved with the sub-epoch ones** — an epoch boundary produces the same path-keyed node tree the sub-epoch window reports do, so every level of the cluster has one stream carrying both cadences: a dense interior filled by window reports, punctuated by epoch rows marked `epoch_complete`. This is the feed that has what the sub-epoch one structurally cannot: **user scalars** (per-rank *and* aggregated — `record_scalar` metrics, eval accuracy) and the **resource sample** (`gpu_util` / `vram_alloc` / `vram_total`, previously always absent from records despite being collected). It also carries a per-node **`label`** — the rank's GPU model, vendor prefixes trimmed — so a legend at any level can read `rank0 · RTX 5060 Ti` rather than just `rank0`, which is the difference that matters on a heterogeneous rig. `EpochMetrics` has no per-rank loss, only the aggregate, so `avg_loss` and `scalars` are injected at the root for keys the per-rank rollup cannot produce; where a key exists both per-rank and aggregated the measured rollup wins, and the two agree by construction (both are batch-weighted means over the same weight). Epoch records land in the persisted record log as well, which until now held sub-epoch rows only.
- **Resources are sampled on a sub-epoch cadence, so a single-epoch run gets a curve instead of a point** — new `TimingMsgWire::ResourceSample`, emitted by each rank's writer thread on a 500 ms wall-clock throttle, independent of the epoch boundary. Resources previously piggy-backed on the per-epoch metrics frame only, which meant a single-pass LLM run — the case `reports_per_epoch` exists for — got **one** GPU/VRAM reading for a run that may last hours. The frame is cheap by construction: `sample()` reads `/proc/stat` plus the already-running NVML poller's accumulator, so this costs a small frame twice a second per rank, not a device query, and it fires on the writer's own tick so a rank blocked in a long barrier keeps reporting. A window record carries `res` only when a **fresh** sample arrived since its last report — repeating the previous value would smear one reading across the epoch, exactly what this cadence exists to avoid — so absent stays absent and the consumer's last-known-per-field view carries the gauge. Both emit paths now share one device-trimming helper so they cannot disagree about which GPU a rank is reporting. Rig-validated on a **1-epoch** 3-rank run: 8 distinct readings per rank (GPU 0.5% → 44.3%, VRAM 0.22 GB → 0.74 GB) where the epoch cadence alone would have produced one.
  - **Fixes a path split between the two feeds.** `from_epoch_metrics` added a host tier whenever host names were known, while the window tree adds one only when the cohort spans more than one host — so on a single-host multi-rank rig the same rank was `root/h1/rank0` in one feed and `root/rank0` in the other, silently splitting its metrics into two paths. Both now go through one shared `cohort_tiering` / `leaf_path_segments` / `rank_record_path`, which the alert lane already used; a rank has exactly one path across every cadence.
  - **`RecordStore` keeps the latest record per path *per cadence*** and answers `/node` with the two folded together — newer fields winning, older filling only what the newer never measured. A single "latest" slot would have let each dense window report blank out the epoch-only fields, so with `reports_per_epoch(20)` a viewer's GPU / VRAM / accuracy gauges would have been empty 19 ticks out of 20 despite having been measured. Record *provenance* (`epoch_complete`) is dropped from the merged snapshot — it describes the row that carried a field, not the node's current state — and stays on the history rows.
  - `NodeRecord::to_record_json` / `flat_records` now take `Option<u64>` for `tick`: an epoch record closes an epoch rather than belonging to a sub-epoch window, so it carries no window index, and `ts` is the axis the two cadences share. `work` is documented as a per-record **interval** quantity whose unit differs by cadence (absolute steps for a window, `batch_share` for an epoch, because `EpochMetrics` carries shares not counts) — exact for every rollup, but not one curve across cadences.
  - Rig-validated on the 3-rank heterogeneous cluster: both cadences interleave in every node's log at one path, epoch rows carry `res` + `label` + the injected root loss, and the live `/node` response shows a window `tick` alongside epoch-only `data_starve` / `res` / `label` in one coherent snapshot (root `vram_total` = the exact sum across all three GPUs, `gpu_util` = the work-weighted mean).
- **Path-addressable record plane on the dashboard port** — `GET /paths`, `GET /node?path=<p>`, `GET /history?path=<p>&n=<N>`, and `GET /stream?path=<p>` (SSE) expose the monitor record stream live, addressed by the same node paths the record log writes on disk. `/node` answers with one **level**: the node's own aggregate plus one record per *direct* child, so a query costs `O(children)` and not `O(cluster)` — a root query on a 1000-rank run returns as much data as one on a 3-rank run, which is what lets a single exposed controller serve any depth (at real cluster scale the workers sit on a private network and are not reachable from a browser at all). Drilling in is just re-scoping the same call. `/history` returns exactly what `/stream` would have sent — the two share one scoping rule — so "read the history, then subscribe" has no gap and no duplicate at the handover, and a `/stream` subscriber's preamble replays the `meta` reduction declarations before any record so it can never interpret one without knowing how it rolls up. Metrics are scoped to depth 1 (a level renders from its direct children, which is what keeps a subscription lean), but **alerts are deliberately not**: a `rank_lost` deep in the tree reaches a `root` subscriber too — you should not have to be looking at the right level to learn a rank died. The original whole-run `/events` epoch feed is untouched and still gets every epoch. New public `flodl::monitor::record_store` (`RecordStore`, `delivers`, `MAX_RECORDS`, `MAX_PATHS`); the live store is bounded (8192-record ring, 4096-path index, and the path cap warns loudly rather than silently dropping). Rig-validated on the 3-rank heterogeneous cluster: `/paths` returned the full six-node index, a `root` subscriber received root + both hosts and **never** a rank while a `root/exa-cuda` subscriber received that host + its rank and nothing from the sibling, and the live `/node` response satisfied the aggregation invariant exactly (root's work-weighted loss == the rollup of its children, 0.0e+00 difference; work and throughput sums exact).
- **Alert lane on the monitor record stream** — rank loss, divergence drift, and dropped control broadcasts now surface as structured `kind: "event"` records alongside the metrics, instead of living only in the `-v` stderr scroll and the in-memory timeline. Each alert carries `class` / `sev` / `detail` / `count` and an **origin `path`** that is exactly the node path the metrics tree uses (`root/<host>/rank2`), so the record log interleaves an alert with the very node's metrics and a future portal can drill straight from the alert to the rank. Three producers are wired: `rank_lost` (critical — every dead-rank detector, carrying the detector's reason), `control_drop` (critical — a best-effort control broadcast that missed live ranks), and `drift` (warn — fired on the **convergence guard's own `NudgeDown` verdict**, not a second coordinator-side threshold that would read a different scale than the configured guard). The lane is bounded by construction and never truncates silently: repeats of the same `(class, path)` inside a 10s window are collapsed, with the first occurrence still emitted **immediately** and the absorbed repeats riding the `count` of the next record past the window (so counts summed over the stream equal the true number of occurrences, with the tail flushed at teardown); at most 200 live entries are retained, and every eviction surfaces in an `overflow` record. Alerts print loudly at the default verbosity whether or not a dashboard is attached — a headless cluster run is where an unnoticed rank death costs the most. New public `flodl::monitor::event_lane` (`EventLane`, `EventClass`, `COLLAPSE_WINDOW_MS`, `MAX_EVENTS`); new `DashboardSink::push_events` (default no-op). Persists automatically wherever `record_log` is enabled — no new knob.
- **Persisted monitor record stream — `Trainer::builder().record_log(dir, max_bytes)`** (ddp-bench: `--record-log DIR`) — the sub-epoch record stream can now be written to disk as it happens, not just served live. A record's `path` **is** its filesystem path, so a run leaves a tree mirroring the cluster (`root.log`, `root/<host>.log`, `root/<host>/rank0.log`, …), one standalone JSON object per line — `jq`-able directly and ingestible by fluentd / GCP Cloud Logging as-is, since every line already carries `ts` / `sev` / `path`. One producer, two sinks: the same records that go live are the lines on disk. Three properties make it safe to leave on for a long run: each node's log is a **drop-oldest ring** capped at `max_bytes` (default 32 MiB, so the tree bounds to `nodes × max_bytes` and a long run cannot fill the disk); every I/O error is **swallowed and warned once**, so a full or read-only disk costs observability and never kills training; and because each `node` record is an absolute snapshot, catching up is a **tail read** of the last N lines (`RecordLog::tail`) rather than an index seek or checkpoint replay. Files stay plain JSONL while live (gzip can be neither appended to nor cheaply tailed); compressing rotated segments is a later additive step. Off by default. New public `flodl::monitor::record_log` (`RecordLog`, `DEFAULT_MAX_LOG_BYTES`). Rig-validated on the 3-rank heterogeneous cluster: the full six-file node tree persisted, and the aggregation invariant was checked against the real output — the root's work-weighted loss equals the rollup through the host tier equals the flat weighted mean over all ranks, to 1e-9, at every tick.
- **Sub-epoch monitor reports — `Trainer::builder().reports_per_epoch(n)`** (ddp-bench: `--reports-per-epoch N`) — training metrics are no longer only per-epoch. The controller emits up to `n` reports per epoch at **reduce boundaries**, giving the loss / throughput curve *between* epoch points; for single-epoch (one-pass LLM) training the per-epoch feed is a single point, so this is the difference between a curve and a dot. The cadence is a work fraction, not a step count: a report fires at the first reduce past each `epoch_work / n` slice of realized work (`epoch_work` = steps per epoch, known ahead from the dataset), so early small windows accumulate across several reduces and the rate settles toward one-per-window as the cadence window grows. It rides the existing single step clock as a read-only observer — reporting never gates, delays, or reschedules a reduce — and reports land only *at* a sync boundary, never mid-window. It costs **zero extra wire traffic**: the per-batch loss already reached the controller on the existing timing frames and was being discarded; it is now accumulated per window (with its own count, deliberately not the step count, which resets on a backend-specific clock). Each report is a path-keyed node tree (`root` → host → rank, collapsing to `root` → rank on a single host) where every interior node aggregates **its direct children only**, cross-rank means are weighted by realized work (so a hierarchical rollup equals the flat one — every node carries its own work), and a metric a rank did not measure this window is **absent**, never zero-filled. `n` is an upper bound: an epoch ending with residual work below the final threshold reports fewer times, which is not a gap because the epoch-boundary point comes from the per-epoch feed — the two feeds compose. Off by default; the per-epoch `metrics_fn` / dashboard contract is unchanged. Controller-path (cluster / auto-promoted multi-GPU) runs. New public vocabulary: `flodl::monitor::record` (the record schema + `Reduction` + the work-weighted tree builder) and `flodl::monitor::cadence` (`ReportScheduler`, `report_interval`); new `DashboardSink::push_window_records` (default no-op). Rig-validated 3-rank heterogeneous (Blackwell + 2×Pascal) on both CPU and NCCL averaging.
- **ddp-bench report: cross-host GPU utilization chart** — a new `gpu-utilization.svg` (grouped horizontal bars: one group per physical GPU `host:device`, one bar per mode) built from the ranks' own host-qualified `rank_samples`, so every host's GPUs are covered — the piece the controller-only `idle-gpu0` bars structurally cannot show. It's the mean compute duty cycle at reduce-window cadence (a mean, not a trajectory). This fills the gap the dedicated-controller poller gate opened: a cluster controller polls no GPU, so `idle-gpu0` (dense, controller-host-only) is silent on cluster runs and this chart carries the utilization story instead; `idle-gpu0` still renders for solo / single-box runs where the controller owns the GPU. Validated on a 3-mode heterogeneous resnet-graph cluster sweep (exa-cuda RTX + 2×Pascal).
- **bf16 wire format for the CPU averaging plane** — `ElCheConfig::bf16_wire(true)` / `Trainer::builder().bf16_wire(true)` (ddp-bench: `--bf16-wire`) ships model traffic (params / buffers) as bfloat16, halving the per-sync payload at every hop: the rank's pinned parameter snapshot (D2H casts on-GPU), the rank→relay frames, the per-host fold shipped upstream, and the scattered consensus. Every accumulator stays f32 — the relay fold and the controller sum decode bf16 at the boundary and re-encode after (`RoundFrame` payloads now carry a bf16 dtype tag alongside f32) — and control traffic (count gathers, the formation broadcast), consensus checkpoints, and the final trained weights remain byte-exact f32. CPU averaging modes only; pairing it with an NCCL mode errors loudly, as does a cohort mixing the setting (frame-schema dtype mismatch at the first fold).
- **Sync-window RAM peak cuts on the CPU reduce path** — three model-sized transients no longer overlap at the reduce barrier (where every rank spikes simultaneously): the pre-scaled contribution scratch is freed the moment the wire frame is encoded, the sent frame is dropped before the blocking reply read, and the reply decodes blob-direct into tensors (no intermediate `Vec<f32>` model copy). Found chasing the first-sync OOM on a RAM-tight two-rank host; applies to f32 and bf16 wires alike.
- **Streaming wire codec on the rank reduce path (both legs)** — a rank's reduce round-trip no longer materializes model-sized wire transients in either direction. Up-leg: the contribution streams straight from parameter staging to the socket, one tensor at a time — frame length computed exactly from shapes + dtype and committed as the length prefix up front, payload bytes MAC'd as written, and the realized-work γ-scale fused into the encode at byte level (bit-identical to the `mul_scalar` it replaces — asserted in tests); the send side drops from ~4 model copies (scaled scratch, payload bytes, and a body serialized TWICE) to ONE tensor's wire bytes. Down-leg: the reduced reply parses straight off the socket (no len-framed blob buffer), each payload draining into its f32 tensor as it arrives with the wire bytes freed per param — reply peak = decoded tensors + one payload, instead of blob + payloads + tensors. Prefix/body length disagreement in either direction is a loud named error (the parse is take-bounded), never a silently desynced stream. At OLMo-190M on the bf16 wire this removes ~2GB of per-rank transients at the sync barrier; measured on a 10GB 2-rank VM, the worst-case headroom floor went 26MB → 341MB with wall time −15%.

- **Incremental relay fold (seed-then-promote)** — the per-host relay now folds each rank's reduce contribution into the round as it arrives (same associative `sum_frames` monoid, computed one deposit at a time; still never divides), instead of holding every local frame until the barrier and summing at the end. The round's first deposit is held verbatim at its wire dtype (the seed — half the f32 image on a bf16 wire, and what the seconds-long barrier hold phase has resident); running f32 sums only materialize when a second deposit arrives. Deposited frames are parsed payload-by-payload and never materialized, the completing deposit re-encodes straight into the uplink payload (draining the accumulator per tensor — no folded frame object, no separate serialize pass), and model-sized mux records ship as header + payload instead of an extra whole-frame copy. A single-rank host ships its seed verbatim without ever touching f32, making the relay tier nearly free on 1-GPU-per-node topologies. On a 2-rank host at OLMo-190M this removes ~1GB of relay-side ship transients per sync window; schema/dtype-mix violations keep failing loudly at the first fold.
- **Host-qualified per-rank resource samples persisted into `timeline.json`** — cluster runs now record every rank's GPU/VRAM/CPU/RAM activity in the saved timeline, not just the controller host's. Ranks already piggy-back resource samples on their metrics reports for the live dashboard; the launcher now requests that sampling whenever its harness carries a `Timeline` (a new opt-in `rank_resources` flag in the rank envelope — headless runs without a timeline still pay no NVML poller), and the coordinator deposits each arriving sample into the timeline tagged with the reporting rank and its host from the world map. `Timeline::save_json` emits them under a new top-level `rank_samples` array (present only when non-empty; unsampled fields are omitted, never zero-filled) and `Timeline::rank_samples()` exposes them in-process. This is the persistence leaf the monitoring portal aggregates later, and the data source for filling the bench report's GPU/VRAM/idle columns for remote hosts (empty until now — the local poller cannot see across hosts). Also fixes the rank-side assigned-device filter for `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES`-scoped rank children: it matched the remapped runtime index against the sampler's physical NVML indices, silently emptying the GPU slice for any rank whose physical device isn't 0 (the dashboard's per-rank GPU tab had the same blind spot).
- **ddp-bench report fills remote-host GPU/VRAM columns from `rank_samples`** — the post-hoc report now consumes the host-qualified per-rank samples persisted into `timeline.json` (see the timeline entry above). The Per-Model table gains a `host:GPUd` column per remote device (sparse mean compute utilization, `~`-marked for the reduce-window cadence it rides), the VRAM table gains per-remote-device peak/mean allocator columns — and prefers the rank-reported allocator bytes even for the controller's own device, since in a multi-process run the controller poller only sees its own (empty) allocator — and the Per-Rank Schedule table gains Host / Util / VRAM-peak columns so every rank is fully described (previously remote ranks showed only allocation share and throughput). Columns key by `(host, device)` because physical device indices collide across hosts; idle analysis stays controller-host-only (the sparse remote cadence cannot support gap detection). The controller's own devices fold into the dense local-poller columns when the launcher can identify itself in the world map — the `timeline.json` gains a top-level `host` stamp for that (present only when the controller box's hostname matches a `cluster.yml` worker name).
- **A dedicated controller no longer reports GPU columns for devices it doesn't train on** — the cluster launcher gates the timeline's local GPU poller off when the controller process owns no local ranks (`local_ranks` empty), so it samples CPU/RAM only and every GPU column in the persisted timeline comes from a worker's own `rank_samples`. This makes a controller-only node behave like a cloud control plane: one column per physical GPU, sourced from the node that owns it, with no dense/rank double-column for a GPU the controller merely happens to see (via a shared container / host-network NVML visibility). When the controller IS co-located with ranks (single-box auto-promote), `local_ranks` is non-empty and its dense high-resolution poll stays authoritative for them (and drives idle-gap analysis). `Timeline::set_gpu_poll(false)` also clears GPU slices from the few samples collected during the membership window before the role is known, keeping CPU/RAM history.
- **`TokenShards::open_raw` + `TokenDtype`** — headerless raw token dumps (what OLMo's preprocessed shards and nanoGPT `.bin` files actually are, `.npy` name notwithstanding), dtype stated explicitly, file length validated against the item size. A prefix slice of a raw dump is itself a valid shard, so partial (range) downloads stage cleanly.
- **`flodl::nn::SwiGLU`** (also re-exported at the crate root, like every other nn layer) — the SwiGLU gate as a module: splits the last dimension in half and returns `value * silu(gate)`, halving the width (`[.., 2n] -> [.., n]`). Deliberately the gate only, with no projections of its own, so it composes between the two a transformer FFN already has (`Linear(d -> 2h) -> SwiGLU -> Linear(h -> d)`) and serves a hand-written `forward` and a `FlowBuilder` graph identically — in a graph it renders as one node instead of an exploded split-and-multiply. **First half is the value, second is the gate**, following OLMo and the fused-projection convention generally; implementations with separate `gate_proj`/`up_proj` reach the same formula from two tensors and so have no ordering to get wrong, but a fused one must pick, and picking the other way trains a different network whose weights are not interchangeable with the reference. Check the chunk order of any source you port a checkpoint from. Errors on an odd last dimension.
- **ddp-bench `olmo-graph` model** — the `olmo` architecture expressed as a `FlowBuilder` graph, the transformer counterpart of the `resnet` / `resnet-graph` pair. A pre-norm block is exactly `also(norm -> attention)` then `also(norm -> ffn)`, since `also()` computes `x + branch(x)` and OLMo has no post-residual activation, so twelve blocks are twenty-four chained calls needing no new graph capability. Shares constants, dataset, loss and optimiser with the eager arm by reference so the two cannot drift on anything except how the forward pass is expressed. Unlike the eager arm it renders as a model diagram; expect a tall one, twelve structurally identical stanzas being an honest picture of a twelve-block transformer.
- **ddp-bench `olmo` model** — OLMo-150M (allenai/OLMo `configs/tiny`) LM pretraining on a staged olmo-mix books slice: RMSNorm + SwiGLU + RoPE decoder (untied head, padded vocab 50,304), AdamW (0.9, 0.95) wd 0.1, warmup + cosine, eval = held-out C4-en CE. Self-controlled via `ddp-bench/scripts/olmo_control.py` (a PyTorch mirror of the same architecture, deviations included) since AI2 publishes no curves for the tiny configs.
- **`flodl::data::datasets::TokenShards`** — pre-tokenized LM corpora in NumPy `.npy` shard files (OLMo / olmo-mix, nanoGPT-style dumps): 1-D token-id arrays (`|u1`/`<u2`/`<u4`/`<i4`/`<i8`), samples are non-overlapping `seq_len` windows with the target shifted by one, windows never cross shard boundaries. Shards are read lazily with positioned reads (no RAM cost up front, index-pure so the staging cascade composes above it); implements both `DataSet` and `BatchDataSet`.
- **`flodl::nn::RotaryEmbedding`** — rotary position embeddings (RoPE) with precomputed sin/cos tables (`on_device_theta` for a custom `rope_theta`), applied to `[batch, heads, seq, head_dim]` query/key pairs; `MultiheadAttention::rotary(...)` attaches one so attention rotates query/key after the head split (LLaMA / OLMo style). Cloning shares the tables, so one instance serves every layer of a deep model.

### Changed

- **`nn::MultiheadAttention` routes its core through fused scaled-dot-product attention** — the explicit `matmul → scale → mask → softmax → matmul` chain held two `[batch, heads, seq_q, seq_k]` tensors (scores and probabilities) through backward; the fused `at::scaled_dot_product_attention` call (whose FFI chain was already shipped end to end, with zero callers in `nn/`) lets libtorch pick flash / memory-efficient / math per device and dtype, and the fused backends never materialize either tensor. At OLMo-150M's seq-256 batch-4 envelope that is ~300MB of VRAM back — ~600MB at batch 8, plausibly the difference that fits batch 8 on a 6GB card (the memory-efficient backend covers sm50+, so Pascal is in). The module's mask contract is unchanged (`true`/non-zero = masked): it converts internally to an **additive** float mask (0 = attend, `-inf` = skip), the exact math `masked_fill` + softmax applied — chosen over inverting to SDPA's boolean convention (`true` = *participate*), whose easy-to-miss inversion is now also documented correctly on `Tensor::scaled_dot_product_attention` (its doc had it backwards; the shim always forwarded straight to libtorch). A test pins the fused core against the verbatim pre-SDPA chain, masked and unmasked. Numerics: the math backend matches the old chain; the fused backends reorder reductions, so loss curves can shift within kernel-level noise — the same class of drift GPU training already has between runs.
- **Mixed-unit metric curves each get the full chart height, with the real axis one click away** — the metrics chart plots series whose units differ by orders of magnitude (`compute_only_ms` runs ~1600× `loss` at the low end), so a shared y-axis handed the height to the largest unit and laid everything else on the floor. Each series now normalizes to its own range on that chart — and only that chart: the resource chart's series are all percentages and the children chart compares one metric across children, real shared scales that normalizing would destroy. Magnitudes are never lost — the tooltip always reads the raw value, the axis labels 0–100% when scaled (absolute labels would lie), and clicking a legend entry isolates a series on its true axis. A zero-span series parks mid-height rather than stretching noise across the canvas; `batch_share` at root is exactly 1.0 by construction, so every root view exercises that guard.
- **The CPU-averaging consensus decodes into reused pinned staging on barrier-paced CUDA ranks, making the averaged-model writeback a true async H2D** — the writeback machinery (comm-stream copies, `copy_done` event, pre-forward fence) was fully async on paper and defeated by one property of its input: the reduce reply decoded into fresh pageable CPU tensors every window, and `cudaMemcpyAsync` from pageable memory silently degrades to a synchronous bounce copy — the GPU-overlap the design promises never actually happened, and a model-sized allocation churned per window per rank. The reduce client now decodes Model-frame replies into per-slot staging buffers, pinned (page-locked) where the platform allows, allocated once and reused for the run: `load_averaged`'s existing non-blocking copies become real `cudaMemcpyAsync`, and the per-window decode alloc disappears (~762MB/window at 190M params, now a one-time locked allocation).
